1

Inception of the Textbook

Good researchers do not work by the seat of their pants, even when it looks that way from the outside. They have run the planning and the process so many times that the steps have gone underground, below conscious thought. This book digs those steps back up so you can learn them on purpose.

Here is what skilled researchers actually do. They ask a question. They find an authoritative source that answers it. They write the answer down in their own words. Then they look at that answer and notice the next gap in what they know, and that gap becomes the next question. Then they do it again. The question drives the whole cycle. The sources are just where you stop along the way.
Most legal research textbooks are really source guides. They tell you what databases exist, how to read an annotated code, how to run a citator check. That is useful, and you will find all of it in this book too. But most of those texts treat the research question as a planning step, something you settle before you begin and then file away once the “real” research starts.

That framing leaves out the most important part. It also blurs two different questions. The question those texts have you settle up front is the big one, the legal issue your finished document must resolve. The questions that drive the actual research are smaller, and there are many of them. Experts research in loops. Each answer raises a new question. Each source exposes a new gap. The inquiry keeps narrowing and deepening until it runs out of questions to ask. When this book says research question, it means those smaller, moving questions; the Introduction lays out the full vocabulary. This is also why you cannot fully plan a research question in advance when you are working in an unfamiliar area of law. You do not yet know which facts matter, or which sources will help, because you do not yet know what the law requires. Relevance is something the cycle uncovers as you go. You cannot load it in at the start.

The Question Method is built on that idea. It puts the iterative question at the center of how you learn legal research, ahead of the database, the source type, and the Bluebook. You will learn to ask a question, find and synthesize an answer, write it down, and ask a sharper question next. You will still learn each of the legal research sources thoroughly, and I would argue you will come out knowing them better, because each chapter shows you how a particular source answers a particular kind of question. A case might give you the elements of a tort. A statute might hand you the definition of a term you have been chasing. A regulation will tell you how to certify produce as organic. A law review article might flag an emerging issue your lobbying clients need you to see coming.

The book also tries to make the invisible visible. An experienced researcher makes dozens of small decisions every session. Which source do I check next? When do I stop reading this case and move on? Is this result actually useful, or does it just look useful? Has this promising lead turned into a dead end? Those decisions are hard to teach precisely because the people who are best at them have stopped noticing they make them. If you already have some research experience, you may make a few of these calls on instinct, and some of what I ask you to do here will feel like a step backward. Take the step anyway. The granularity is scaffolding, and scaffolding comes down. Work every step explicitly now, and you will compress the chain later, the way every expert eventually does, knowing exactly which steps you are skipping because you once performed each one on purpose.  If you are new to this, the book will help you build that judgment one deliberate move at a time. Work through it carefully, and you will come away with more than a map of where the law lives. You will have a practiced way of thinking about how to find the law, how to use it, and how to know when you are finished.

2

Introduction

A few years ago, in the middle of a legal research class, a student asked me a question that changed how I teach. We had spent the session on secondary sources: how to find them, how to navigate a table of contents, how to scan a section’s footnotes for the citations that point to cases on your issue. We had even worked through a passage together and flagged the footnotes worth chasing. Then the student looked at the half-dozen cases those footnotes pointed to and asked, “But which one do I read first?”

It was a simple question. It was also exactly the right one, because it exposed a gap, not in what the student understood, but in what I had taught. I had shown them how to find a secondary source, how to navigate it, and how to spot the passages whose footnotes led somewhere useful. I had not shown them how to decide which lead to follow first. I had assumed that choice was obvious. It is not, and it happens to be the judgment that separates a novice from an expert. Handed six plausible citations, a novice reads all of them and drowns, or picks one at random and hopes. An expert reads the one that answers the question they are holding right now. The footnotes do not put themselves in order. The researcher’s current question orders them. Knowing which gap to close next, and therefore which lead to chase first, is the skill that source-guide instruction tends to leave out. This textbook is what happened when I took that realization seriously.

The Question Method in Brief

The Question Method is a structured, repeating approach to legal research. The cycle is simple. You define the question you are working on right now. You research to answer it. You write down what you found. Based on what you found, you identify the next question. Then you run the cycle again, and you keep going until no real question is left. None of this is new. It is what skilled researchers already do. The method just names each move so you can learn it on purpose instead of hoping to absorb it by osmosis.

Naming each move is what makes it teachable. Because every step has a name, you can watch yourself do it, and you can catch yourself when it goes wrong. When the research starts to feel unproductive, you can stop and ask, “What kind of question am I actually asking right now?” More often than not you will find the question is poorly scoped, pointed at the wrong source, or already answered. Naming the moves also answers my student’s question. When a source hands you several leads at once, the one to follow first is the one that answers your current question. The rest become later questions, which you write down and come back to in turn. (Appendix A returns to this exact moment when it shows you how to work a secondary source’s footnotes.) The method threads that discipline through every stage of research and writing, so the judgment an expert applies without thinking becomes a habit you build on purpose.

One Issue, Many Questions

Before the method gets to work, three terms need pinning down, because the rest of this book leans on them. The legal issue is the problem the law must resolve, the reason the file landed on your desk. The macro question is that issue framed as the single big question your document exists to answer: the question presented in a memo, the issue on appeal in a brief, the thesis of a seminar paper. A research question is the micro question you ask and answer along the way, one of the many subquestions whose answers, assembled, answer the macro question. When this book says research question, it always means the micro question.

The distinction matters because the macro question is a terrible research companion. If it is the only question you hold, every source has to be weighed against all of it at once, and every passage looks potentially relevant, because everything ultimately bears on the macro question. That is not how brains process information. We work through one concept at a time. Nobody learns a dance routine by running the whole thing at every rehearsal. You drill it in sections, then you assemble the performance. Research questions are the sections.

This is also why it is not enough to write a research question at the planning stage and never revisit it. A researcher who does that is really carrying the macro question through the whole project, trying to process everything a source says all at once, and feeling buried by it. The method asks for the opposite discipline: write the macro question down where you can see it, hold exactly one research question at a time, and let each answer hand you the next. The macro question is the destination. The research questions are the steps.

How to Use This Textbook

If you are in a first-year legal research course, read Part I straight through, in order. The method shows up in Chapter 3 and runs through every chapter after it. Start each chapter with its Chapter Objectives. They tell you what you should be able to do by the end, not just what you should know. The Research Log is the single most useful practical tool in this book. Use it for every exercise here, and eventually for every research task you take on in practice. The habit you build in the safety of a course is the same one that will protect you and your clients later.

3

Use of AI in the Development of this Textbook

Generative AI was used in the development of this textbook. This section is my effort to disclose that use as transparently as possible, so you can be as informed as possible as you engage with the material. Please note, I reviewed and edited every chapter before publication, and I verified every statement of law and every citation against primary authority, the same rule Chapter 15 asks of you. Any errors that remain are mine. If you find one, tell me. That’s the beauty of a live, open-access textbook: I can fix it.

How did I Use GenAI?

I used generative AI to help build this textbook and the accompanying interactive website and instructor edition, from early planning through final drafts. The ideas and frameworks are mine. The Question Method deviates sharply from the methodology most research textbooks describe. So, this textbook could not be created solely with a prompt and limited oversight.  The book is built on a large body of my original work product: teaching notes, methodological descriptions, fully worked examples, chapter outlines, draft language for the core elements of each chapter, and samples of my writing so the drafting would sound like me. AI drafted from that foundation.I also matched the tool to the task rather than defaulting to the most powerful option: the smallest capable models for settled material and first drafts, larger models only where the work demanded them, for developing novel concepts at scale, for deep structural edits, and vibe coding to speed the development of the accompanying website.

Why Did I Use GenAI?

I did not make the choice to utilize Generative AI lightly.  I weighed the costs of using this tool, which I attempt to calculate below, against the value of a free, open textbook on a new method with a companion interactive website finished within a few months before the Fall 2026 semester.  As with many libraries, our bandwidth is limited, and our desire to do the absolute best for the communities we serve is often bottlenecked by that bandwidth. As such, this tool gave me the ability to expand my internal teaching materials on this method into a full textbook that could better serve my students and hopefully students at other institutions.

Estimated Impact

It was my intention from the beginning to limit the use of genAI to initial development. Nothing about reading, hosting, or updating this book will utilize another large language model.  The accompanying interactive website might, but only if there are major technological issues that must be addressed within a timeframe too short for me to programmatically solve myself in a timely manner (e.g., a major accessibility concern). I can and intend to update the content and code myself as needed. Below are my best estimates of the environmental impact from the development of this textbook and accompanying resources. These numbers are in flux as people continue to do research, so I used the high end for my textbook and the median of ranges for the conventional textbook.

  • Using the high end of available estimates, developing this book and companion materials consumed roughly 12 kWh of energy (up to 12 kg of CO2, depending on the grid) and about 100 liters of water. Digital hosting adds an estimated 3 to 7 kWh and 1.3 to 3.1 kg of CO2 per gigabyte per year, and this book is a fraction of a gigabyte (I only know the size of the downloadable files, not the actual size of all code on the Pressbooks site).
  • For comparison, a conventional 400-page print textbook is estimated to consume 400 liters of water (over 5,000 at the high end), about 2 kg of timber (roughly a twentieth of a tree), and 2.7 to 7.5 kg of CO2 across its life cycle. This is per textbook.

What is harder to quantify, and deeply important to me, is the positive impact from students who pay nothing for their class materials, and, I hope, students who learn legal research better from the question method and more than one way to learn it.

The development costs for this textbook were paid once, for every reader. At least 40 students will be using this textbook this year between my fall and spring classes.  To me, if this textbook can improve legal research comprehension, even among this small a cohort, the positive impact outweighs the negative externalities. Should it be adopted by others, which is the hope of any textbook writer, the cost per user shrinks, meaning the positive impact begins to exponentially outweigh the negative externalities.

I

Part I: Foundational Legal Research Topics

Part I is the complete foundation: everything you need to complete most basic research projects from first question to documented closure. Chapter 1 maps where the law lives, because you cannot decide where to look until you know what is out there. Chapter 2 introduces the platforms and search mechanics you will use every working day. Then Chapter 3 introduces the Question Method itself, the engine the rest of this book runs on. Read those three in order. Everything after them assumes you have.

Chapters 4 through 7 each take up one major source type: secondary sources, case law, statutes, and regulations. They share a deliberate template. Each shows you how the source fits the research cycle, what kinds of questions it answers, how it is made and what that means for your research, and how to search it. The repetition is the point. By the fourth pass, the questions you ask of a new source will start to feel automatic, and that is the habit forming.

Chapters 8 and 9 put the pieces together. Real projects rarely live in one source; Chapter 8 shows you how statutes, regulations, and cases interlock, and how to run the cycle across all of them at once. Chapter 9 teaches the endgame: precision searching once your questions have narrowed, and the closing-out protocol that lets you say, with a documented basis, that the research is done. Between the macro question you write down at the start and the closure you document at the end, every move in this part is one you will use for the rest of your career.

A word on how to work through it. The chapters include drills, and Appendix E holds the model walkthroughs, kept apart so you cannot read an answer by accident. Attempt every drill before you look. The method only becomes yours through repetition, and Part I is where you get them. Do the reps.

1

Foundations of Legal Authority

Before you can research well, you have to know where answers are likely to live. That means understanding how the U.S. legal system is structured and what primary sources of law govern. Think of this chapter as the map you study before the trip. Every research question you ask in this course eventually lands on one or more of the primary sources described here, and knowing how those sources relate to one another is what lets you make smart decisions about where to look. A researcher who does not understand the hierarchy of authority cannot tell a source that settles a question from one that merely informs it, and will burn time reading material that could never control the outcome. This chapter is also the ground the Question Method stands on. When Chapter 3 asks you to write a research question and choose the source most likely to answer it, the choice will come from the US legal structure you are about to study.

Learning Objectives

Type your learning objectives here.

  • Identify the sources of primary legal authority in the United States
  • Describe how the law on a single topic can come from an overlapping set of constitutional provisions, statutes, regulations, and case law
  • Use the structure of the U.S. legal system to plan your research
  • Distinguish mandatory (controlling) from persuasive authority in a given jurisdiction
  • Apply the hierarchy of legal authority to determine which source governs a particular issue

Sources of U.S. Law

The United States has a federal system, which means governing power is shared between the federal government and the states. Each level has its own constitution, its own legislature, its own agencies, and its own courts. The law itself comes from a handful of primary sources: constitutions, statutes, regulations, case law, and treaties. Knowing what each one is, and what each one can and cannot do, is the first step toward knowing where to look for an answer.

Constitutions set up the structure of government, define the limits of government power, and guarantee individual rights. A constitution is the supreme law of its jurisdiction, so any other law that conflicts with it is invalid. The U.S. Constitution structures the federal government and, through the Fourteenth Amendment and the doctrine of incorporation, also limits much of what state governments may do. Each state also has its own constitution, supreme within that state so long as it does not conflict with federal law.

Statutes are laws passed by a legislature, such as the U.S. Congress or a state legislature. Once enacted, they are first published as a standalone document called a slip law. They are later gathered chronologically and published in bound volumes called session laws. Then they are organized by subject into collections called codes, like the United States Code for federal law. A statute is the considered, generally applicable judgment of the elected legislature, and it can create rights, impose duties, prohibit conduct, and set up entire regulatory schemes.

Regulations are rules created by government agencies, like the Environmental Protection Agency or the Department of Labor, under authority a statute delegated to them. Regulations fill in the technical detail that statutes leave to the agencies. A statute might direct an agency to set “safe” exposure limits for a workplace chemical; the regulation supplies the actual number. Properly adopted regulations carry the force of law.

Case law is the body of published judicial opinions.When an appellate-level judge writes and publishes an opinion, that opinion generally becomes part of the law, binding future lower courts in the same jurisdiction under the doctrine of stare decisis. (There is some nuance here that you’ll find covered in Chapter 5.) Some case law is common law, rules courts created where no statute governs. For example, much of tort law comes from judicial decisions alone. The rest of case law interprets and applies constitutions, statutes, or regulations.

Treaties are formal agreements between the United States and foreign or tribal nations. Once ratified, a treaty has the force of federal law and can preempt conflicting state law or older federal law.

The Interplay of U.S. Legal Authorities

The U.S. legal system grew out of the English common law tradition, where law developed case by case through judicial decision. Over time, legislatures began codifying areas of law, which produced the modern dual structure of judge-made and legislatively enacted law.

A few domains, like negligence, defamation, contract formation, and much of property law, remain governed mostly by common law because their principles are flexible and have been refined over centuries of judicial decision-making. Courts have proven better suited than legislatures to the incremental, fact-sensitive elaboration these doctrines need. Other areas, like environmental protection, securities regulation, tax, or immigration, demand comprehensive statutory frameworks and agency oversight, because they require uniformity, technical precision, and the ability to update rules as circumstances change. In practice, most modern legal issues pull in all three at once: statutes set the framework, regulations supply the detail, and case law interprets and applies both.

Because power is split between federal and state governments, a single issue might be governed by federal law, state law, or both, depending on the subject. It might also involve statutes, case law, and regulations at one or both levels at the same time. An employment dispute, for example, might implicate a federal statute (Title VII), federal regulations (EEOC rules), federal case law interpreting both, a parallel state anti-discrimination statute, and a state common law claim like wrongful discharge, all together.

So, as you research, you have to keep all of the authorities that might apply in view. This is one reason secondary sources (Chapter 4) are so useful early on. They can describe the whole landscape of an area and point you to the relevant authorities, which saves you from tracking them all down yourself and protects you from the dangerous assumption that the first source you find is the only one that matters.

These sources also act on each other. Statutes create agencies, which then write regulations. Courts interpret both statutes and regulations, and they fill gaps with common law where no statute applies. Courts can strike down regulations they find invalid, and legislatures can pass new statutes that override a court’s reading of a statute. Courts can also strike down statutes that conflict with the Constitution. The result is a moving system in which authority is constantly being created, interpreted, revised, and occasionally invalidated.

Examples of complex areas of U.S. law:

  • Environmental regulation. Congress passes the Clean Air Act (a statute). The EPA writes emissions standards under that Act (regulations). Courts interpret the statute and the regulations when disputes arise (case law). No single source tells the whole story; the rule that governs a particular smokestack emerges only from reading all three together.
  • Tax law. In Rowan Companies, Inc. v. United States, 452 U.S. 247 (1981), the Supreme Court struck down Treasury regulations that interpreted “wages” inconsistently for different tax purposes. Congress responded by amending the Internal Revenue Code through the Social Security Amendments of 1983 to allow exactly the differentiation the Court had rejected, effectively reversing the decision through new legislation. It is a textbook illustration of the legislature having the last word over a court’s reading of a statute.
  • Constitutional law. In Texas v. Johnson, 491 U.S. 397 (1989), the Supreme Court struck down a law criminalizing flag burning, holding that it was symbolic speech protected by the First Amendment. Here the court had the last word, because the conflict was between a statute and the Constitution rather than between a court and a legislature over a statute’s meaning.

This constant interplay is why you always have to check that your authority is still valid. A statute you plan to cite might have been struck down by a court. A case you want to rely on might have been superseded by a new statute or overruled by a higher court. A regulation might have been invalidated or amended. Verifying all of this is part of competent research, and later chapters treat it as a required step rather than a courtesy.

[Figure 1.1] Flowchart of the cyclical interaction of authorities: legislature creates a statute, agency creates a regulation, court interprets both, court may invalidate, legislature may respond with a new statute. Show feedback loops among all three branches.

The Hierarchy of Legal Authority

Primary vs. Secondary Authority

Primary authority is the law itself: constitutions, statutes, regulations, case law, and treaties. These are what courts rely on to decide cases. Secondary sources explain, interpret, or analyze the law, but they hold no authority to bind government or citizen action. They are like road maps that help you find where you need to go, but they are not the destination. In most court filings you will cite primary authorities, not secondary sources, with a few exceptions later chapters cover. For research, the practical consequence is that secondary sources are usually where you start and primary authorities are usually where you end. You read the map to find the road, then you travel the road.

Binding (Mandatory/Controlling) vs. Persuasive Authority

Not all primary authority carries the same weight. Some of it is binding (also called mandatory or controlling), meaning a court must follow it. Some of it is persuasive, meaning a court may be influenced by it but is not required to follow it. Telling the two apart is one of the most consequential judgments a researcher makes, because it decides whether a source ends your inquiry or merely informs it.

Whether a primary authority binds depends on jurisdiction. An authority binds your court only when it comes from home: your jurisdiction’s own constitution, statutes, and regulations, the decisions of the courts above yours in the same system, and the federal law that applies there. California’s state laws have no binding effect in Arizona. For courts specifically, a judicial opinion binds only if it comes from a higher court in the same court system.

Run one state through the whole structure. An Arizona trial court must follow the Arizona Supreme Court on issues of Arizona law. A federal district court sitting in Arizona must follow Ninth Circuit precedent on issues of federal law. But when that same federal court has to decide a question of Arizona law, the direction of authority reverses: it takes its cue from Arizona’s own courts, especially the Arizona Supreme Court, because Arizona’s courts are the final word on what Arizona law means. (Exactly how federal courts predict state law is a wrinkle with rules of its own; we will leave those to your civ pro class.) All must comply with U.S. Supreme Court decisions interpreting the U.S. Constitution.

Persuasive authority is everything else: primary authorities from other jurisdictions, decisions from courts at the same level, decisions from lower courts, and all secondary sources. A court is not required to follow persuasive authority, but it is more likely to when the issue is new or unsettled in the controlling jurisdiction, when another court’s reasoning is particularly strong, or when there is a general trend of the law changing across the country.  Persuasive authority matters most when you are arguing for a position that is unsettled. Say no Arizona court has ever addressed whether drone photography can violate someone’s privacy. An Arizona judge might look at how courts in New Mexico or California handled similar cases. Those decisions would not bind the court, but they could persuade it, and the more closely the other jurisdiction’s legal framework resembles Arizona’s, the more persuasive they become.

Persuasion can also travel further than a single case. The shift from contributory to comparative negligence in tort law shows how persuasive authority can gradually reshape binding law. The change started slowly, was advocated in secondary sources like the Restatement of Torts, was adopted by a few courts, and eventually became the majority rule across the country. What began as merely persuasive became, jurisdiction by jurisdiction, binding.

In practice: Watching Persuasive Become Binding. California’s switch is a clean specimen. For decades the state followed contributory negligence, under which a plaintiff even slightly at fault recovered nothing. Nothing binding required a change. What existed was persuasive material: scholarly criticism, other states’ decisions, and comparative rules already in force elsewhere. In Li v. Yellow Cab Co., 13 Cal. 3d 804 (1975), the California Supreme Court drew on exactly that body of non-binding authority and replaced the rule with comparative fault. The moment the opinion issued, the persuasive material had done its work and the new rule was binding on every court in the state. If you had researched this question in 1974, the best answer would have been “contributory negligence controls, but the persuasive weight is running the other way,” and that second clause is the one a client needed.

Reflection Point. When would a persuasive decision be worth more to your client than a binding one? (A short answer appears in Appendix X.)

Federal Preemption

Under the Supremacy Clause of the U.S. Constitution (Article VI), federal law overrides conflicting state law in areas Congress is empowered by the Constitution to pass laws on, such as patent law and large parts of immigration law. This is called preemption, and it can be express (Congress says so directly) or implied (the structure or purpose of the federal scheme leaves no room for state regulation). In areas where federal law sets only a floor, though, such as many environmental or labor protections, states may pass laws stricter than the federal requirement. Whenever you research a state-law question in an area that is also federally regulated, ask whether federal law preempts the state rule before you rely on it.

Research Implications

A solid grasp of this structure lets you do three things:

  • Decide where to begin. Which level of law governs this issue? If the area is comprehensively codified, start with the statute. If it is common-law territory, start with cases. If you are not sure, start with a secondary source that will tell you.
  • Sort binding from persuasive sources. Which authorities must you follow, and which can you cite for support? That judgment sets how much weight to give each source and how hard to keep searching for something better.
  • Track how sources interact. How do statutes, regulations, and cases shape one another? A complete answer often means assembling several sources rather than finding a single one.

Working this way keeps your arguments grounded in the right authorities, which is most of what people mean when they call a memo well researched. It also feeds directly into the Question Method introduced in Chapter 3: every cycle there begins by asking which authority can answer the question at hand, and this structure is where that answer comes from.

Common Pitfalls

The mistake that does the most damage early on is treating the first source you find as the whole answer. A single statute or case rarely tells the full story when regulations and judicial interpretation are also in play, and stopping too soon leaves a gap exactly where the governing rule lives. A related error is confusing persuasive authority for binding authority, then building an argument on a case your court has no obligation to follow. Watch the jurisdiction line carefully, because a perfectly good California decision does nothing for you in an Arizona court. And do not skip the validation step. Authority that was good law last year may have been amended, overruled, or struck down since, and citing it as though it still controls is the kind of error that follows you into practice.

Chapter Recap

U.S. law comes from five primary sources (constitutions, statutes, regulations, case law, and treaties), layered across one federal system and fifty state systems. Within any jurisdiction, the hierarchy runs constitution over statute, statute over regulation, with case law interpreting all three or, where no statute governs, supplying the rule itself as common law. And for any given court, an authority is either binding, meaning the court must follow it, or persuasive, meaning the court can listen and still say no.  This structure is what will inform where you look to answer your research questions.

Key Takeaways

  • U.S. law comes from five interconnected primary sources: constitutions, statutes, regulations, case law, and treaties.
  • Most modern issues involve multiple types of authority at once.
  • The hierarchy of authority determines which source controls: constitutions over statutes, statutes over regulations, and so on.
  • Mandatory authority is law a court must follow; persuasive authority is law a court may consider but is not required to follow.
  • Read the law as interlocking parts of a system that is always evolving, which is why validating every authority is a required step, not an optional one.

Reviewing Through Practice

The following exercises test your understanding of which sources of authority are involved in different scenarios and why. No searching is required.

Exercise 1.1 — The Layered-Authority Fact Pattern. A client comes to you with this: she was fired from her job at a private company in your state after a routine drug test detected marijuana. She has a prescription-backed medical authorization to use it under your state’s law; she used it only at home and never worked impaired; and her employee handbook says nothing about the situation either way. She wants to know whether the firing was legal. Identify every layer of authority that might bear on it. Work through the chapter’s map deliberately: could a federal statute be involved? A state statute? An agency’s regulations at either level? Judge-made common law? A constitutional provision? For each layer you identify, write one sentence on what that layer might contribute to the eventual answer.

Deliverable: a table with one row per authority, three columns: the level of government (federal, state, or local), the type of source (constitution, statute, regulation, case law), and your one-sentence prediction of its role.

Exercise 1.2 — Binding or Persuasive? A trial court in Arizona is hearing a negligence claim, an area the chapter told you is governed mostly by state common law. The parties have put seven authorities in front of the court. Classify each as binding or persuasive for the Arizona trial court, with one sentence explaining your reasoning, and for the authorities you classified as persuasive, when a court might choose to follow the authority:

  1. a decision of the Arizona Supreme Court on the duty at issue;
  2. a decision of the Arizona Court of Appeals on the same duty;
  3. a decision of the California Supreme Court on nearly identical facts
  4. a decision of a different Arizona trial court; (e) a decision of a federal district court sitting in Arizona discussing Arizona negligence law
  5. a section of a well-regarded treatise on negligence;
  6. a decision of the U.S. Supreme Court interpreting the U.S. Constitution on a point raised in the case.

Deliverable: the seven classifications with justifications and explanations.

Exercise 1.3 — Which version governs? When reading a case for your research, you come across this quote: “In cases, like this, where the two versions conflict, the rule is that the Statutes at Large version controls.” Midland Power Co-op. v. Federal Energy Regulatory Com’n, 774 F.3d 1, at 3 (D.C. Cir 2014) (finding discrepancy between 16 U.S.C. 825l(b) & 49 Stat. 860 that arose when the publisher changed a word when adding it to the US Code). The court was comparing a statute as Congress enacted it, 49 Stat. 860 (using “under this Act.”), with the same statute as it appears in the United States Code, 16 U.S.C. § 825l(b)(using under this chapter). The wording of the two is not identical. In two or three sentences, using only what the chapter told you about how a statute is made and published, explain why the enacted version might control over the codified version.
Deliverable: one paragraph, three to five sentences.

Sample answers for this chapter’s exercises appear in Appendix X.

2

Introduction to Legal Research Platforms

Legal research platforms are commercial interfaces that index and make searchable the primary and secondary authorities that courts, legislatures, and agencies produce. The three major commercial platforms are Westlaw, Lexis+, and Bloomberg Law, and each provides its own research tools and editorial enhancements. Free alternatives, including Google Scholar, CourtListener, eCFR.gov, GovInfo.gov, and Congress.gov, provide access to many primary sources, though with fewer research tools than the commercial platforms and, often, less complete coverage of older or unreported material.

Each platform organizes its content into searchable collections such as by source type (cases, statutes, regulations, secondary sources) and by jurisdiction. Before you run any search, set the appropriate jurisdiction and source-type filters. This one step saves an enormous amount of time by keeping irrelevant results out of your way. A search for “reasonable accommodation” run across all jurisdictions and all source types returns an unusable wall of results; the same search confined to Ninth Circuit cases returns a set you can actually read.

This chapter is about how the platforms work and the mechanics of searching them (how to navigate and get filtered results). It is deliberately kept apart from the strategy of searching (what to search for and how to recognize the best source), which is the subject of Chapter 3’s Question Method and the source-specific chapters that follow. Learning the mechanics first means that when strategy questions come up, you already know how to execute the search the strategy calls for.

Learning Objectives

  • Identify the major legal research platforms and their core features
  • Distinguish between natural language searching and terms-and-connectors (Boolean) searching
  • Build basic searches using the core Boolean connectors AND, OR, and NOT
  • Use expansion operators (! and *) to capture different forms of the same word
  • Apply jurisdiction and source-type filters to focus your results
  • Select when to use a broad search and when to use a narrow one

Understanding the Common Features

Databases change, and they change often. Interfaces get redesigned, features get renamed, and new tools appear every year. A few features, though, are common across platforms and stable over time: a central search box, the ability to filter by jurisdiction and source type, a citator for validating authority, and some system of topical classification. If you learn the underlying concepts rather than the location of a particular button, your skills will carry from one platform to the next and survive the next interface redesign.

Sample database framework showing the central search box, the help icon, and the topical tabs.

Citations and Finding Known Sources

Reading a Legal Citation

A legal citation tells you three things: what type of source it is, where it was published, and when it was published. A case citation typically appears as Party v. Party, Volume Reporter Page (Court Year). The reporter and volume tell you where the opinion is published; the court and year, in the parenthetical, tell you immediately how much authority the case might carry in your jurisdiction.

Case Citation

Robles v. Domino’s Pizza, LLC,Case Name (Caption)
913Volume
F.3dReporter
898,First Page
(9th Cir.Court
2019)Year Decided

A statute citation identifies the title and section of the code where the law lives: the title number, the code abbreviation, and the section symbol followed by the section number.

Statute Citation

42Title
U.S.C.Code
§ 12182Section
(a)Subsection
(2018)Edition Year

A regulation citation follows the same logic as a statute but points to the Code of Federal Regulations rather than a statutory code.

Regulation Citation

28Title
C.F.R.Code
§ 36.303Section
(a)Subsection
(2023)Edition Year

[Figure 2.2] Example case, statute, and regulation citations, each with arrows labeling its parts.

Finding the Cited Source

When you already have a citation, you are able to retrieve the authority in a legal database by entering relevant portions into the search bar. Enter only the location portions of the citation (volume, reporter, page, or title and section), not the party names or the year. The location portions are what the database indexes; the names and year are descriptive and can actually interfere with a clean retrieval.

Searching for Authorities

When you do not already have a citation, when you do not yet know what you want to find, you have to search the database to discover sources that might be relevant. What to search for and how to spot the best results are covered in later chapters. This section is about the mechanics of searching a legal research system.

There are two basic ways to search a legal database, and choosing between them is one of the first decisions you make when you sit down to research. Many systems have added semantic searching through generative AI chat interfaces. These are covered in Chapter 15, but are best used after you understand and are able to make the critical judgments required when using the basic ways to search described below.

Natural language searching lets you type a description of your issue in plain terms, almost like asking a question. The database uses algorithms to rank results by how closely they match what you typed. It is a good way to start when you are still exploring and do not yet know the precise legal terminology, because it does not require you to know the magic words. It tolerates ordinary language and returns the documents it judges most relevant.

Terms-and-connectors searching (also called Boolean searching) is more precise. Instead of letting the database guess what you want, you tell it exactly what to look for: which words must appear, which words must be near each other, which words to exclude. This is the right tool when you know what you are looking for and need to be sure you find everything relevant, because a well-built Boolean search returns every document that satisfies its logic and nothing that does not.

Most research starts with natural language and moves to Boolean precision as your questions get more specific. You do not have to choose one over the other; they work well together. Chapter 9 covers the full precision toolkit once your research has narrowed. This chapter introduces the foundation you will build on there.

Core Terms and Connectors

  • AND: both terms must appear somewhere in the document. Use it to require your concepts to co-occur. Example: landlord AND habitability. A document mentioning only one of the two will not be retrieved.
  • OR: either term may appear. Use it for synonyms and alternate ways of describing the same concept. A good search for “landlord” might include landlord OR lessor OR owner. This widens your results to catch sources that use different words for the same idea, and missing a synonym is one of the most common ways researchers miss relevant authority.
  • NOT (or BUT NOT): the first term must appear; the second must not. Use it to drop an irrelevant context. Example: marijuana NOT prescription. Use NOT carefully, because it removes every document containing the excluded term, and it can quietly discard relevant results whenever the excluded term happens to co-occur with your issue.
  • Quotation marks: the exact phrase must appear in exactly that order. Example: “reasonable accommodation”. This is your most powerful precision tool when you know the exact legal standard you are after, because it cuts out the noise of the two words appearing separately and unrelated.

Expansion Operators

! (root expander) retrieves all words sharing the same root. Example: employ! retrieves employ, employee, employer, employment, and employed all at once. This keeps you from missing results because a source used a slightly different form of your term. Be careful, though, because the expander grabs words you did not intend. bit! retrieves bite, biter, bitten, and biting, but also bitter, bitcoin, and bittersweet. The longer and more distinctive the root, the safer the expansion.

* (single-character wildcard) fills exactly one character position. Example: wom*n retrieves woman and women. Use it when a word has common variant spellings or inflections that a root expander would not catch.

Practical Search Strategy

A search string is your current research question translated into the database’s language, which is why a vague question produces a vague search. Two habits make the difference between a search that works and one that buries you.

First, before you run anything, brainstorm both the lay term and the legal term for each concept: fired and terminated and discharged; or landlord and lessor; or lease and rental agreement. Lawyers, judges, and ordinary people describe the same thing in different words, and the source you need may use a word you did not think of. Use OR to gather all the variants for a single concept in one search.

Second, connect your distinct concepts with AND so the results discuss all of them at once. A search like (fired OR terminated OR discharged) AND (disab! OR “medical condition”) requires both a separation concept and a disability concept to appear, which is far more useful than searching either one alone.

Start broad. Connecting two or three core concepts with AND gives you a manageable set while you are still orienting yourself, and it forgives the fact that you do not yet know the precise terminology. As your questions get more specific, moving from exploratory to focusing to application, your searches should narrow to match, adding the precision tools from Chapter 9: proximity connectors (/n, /s, /p), frequency operators (ATLEAST), and the strategies for closing out a project. The progression of your searches should track the progression of your questions.

[Figure 2.4] Side-by-side comparison of the same research question searched by natural language and by Boolean, with sample result counts showing how each approach behaves.

Reflection Point. Why does the method have you draft the research question before you choose a database or a search syntax? (A short answer appears in Appendix I.)

 

Common Pitfalls

The most frequent platform mistake is searching before setting the jurisdiction and source-type filters, which floods you with results from courts and source types that could never answer your question. After that comes leaning too hard on a single set of search terms; if you only ever search for “fired,” you will miss every case that says “terminated” or “discharged,” so brainstorm synonyms and join them with OR. NOT deserves its own caution, because it is easy to exclude a word that sometimes travels with the authority you actually want and quietly delete good results in the process. And the root expander rewards a little restraint: a short root like bit! drags in bitcoin and bittersweet, along with the biting and bitten, so reach for the longest, most distinctive root that still captures the variants you need.

Chapter Recap

Legal research platforms are the infrastructure you use to reach primary and secondary authority. Understanding the difference between natural language and Boolean searching, and knowing when to use each, is the foundation of efficient research. Natural language gets you moving when you are unsure of the vocabulary; Boolean gives you control once you know what you are after. The expansion operators and connectors here form the basis of the precision toolkit you will develop in Chapter 9.

Key Takeaways

  • Westlaw, Lexis+, and Bloomberg Law are the major commercial platforms; free alternatives provide access to many primary sources but with fewer tools and less complete coverage.
  • Natural language searching suits exploration; Boolean searching suits precision retrieval.
  • Brainstorm lay and legal synonyms and join them with OR; connect distinct concepts with AND.
  • Always set jurisdiction and source-type filters before searching.

Reviewing Through Practice

The following exercises test your ability to translate a question into a search, compare search modes, and retrieve known sources from their citations. Before every search, record the jurisdiction and source-type filters you set and why.

Exercise 2.1 — Translate the Question. A mail carrier was bitten by a customer’s dog while delivering a package, and she wants to know whether the dog’s owner is liable for her injuries. Identify the distinct concepts inside that question. There are at least three. For each concept, build a synonym set of at least three terms joined by OR, then connect the concept sets with AND into a single terms-and-connectors search. Watch for the root-expander trap the chapter warned about: a short root drags in everything that shares its letters, so test every truncated term against what it would actually sweep up.
Deliverable: your concept list, the synonym set for each, and the final search string. 

Exercise 2.2 — Two Modes, One Question. Run the dog-bite question twice on the same platform: once as a natural language search, in a plain sentence, and once as a terms-and-connectors search, built as this chapter taught, synonym sets joined by OR for each concept, concepts connected with AND. Set your jurisdiction filter to your state and your source-type filter to cases before either search, and record both. Compare the first ten results of each run.
Deliverable: your search notes, both queries as entered, both filter sets, and three sentences on how the result lists differ: what each mode surfaced that the other missed, and which you would trust to start this project.

Exercise 2.3 — Retrieve, Don’t Search. If you already have a citation, you do not search. You retrieve. Here are three citations, one case, one statute, one regulation: Hazelwood School District v. Kuhlmeier, 484 U.S. 260 (1988); 42 U.S.C. § 12101; and 29 C.F.R. § 1605.1. [Cite-check note: verify these three retrieval targets, or substitute instructor-preferred citations; the exercise works with any three so long as the source types vary.] For each, first label the citation’s anatomy, every location part and what it tells you, and then pull the document using only those location parts. No words in any search bar.
Deliverable: the three labeled anatomies and, for each document, the retrieval path you used.

Sample answers for this chapter’s exercises appear in Appendix X.

 

3

The Question Method of Legal Research

Legal research is a moving series of questions that continues until you find the answer you need or must stop for practical reasons. Collecting sources is easy; a pile of printouts is not research. The skill is knowing what you need at every stage.

Good researchers pause and ask themselves things like: What type of question am I asking now? Is this the right level of inquiry for where I am? What source can best answer it? To a beginner, those pauses feel like interruptions. That is because it is not yet instinctive as it is for experts, and so it feels like an extra step.

The Question Method builds the those pauses into the process: craft a question, research the answer, synthesize the answer into your writing notes, and craft the next question. With a well-crafted question in hand, you will find where to look faster, understand what you find more clearly, spot gaps and ambiguities sooner, and know with some confidence when your research is actually done.

Learning Objectives

  • Articulate the Question Method and distinguish it from passive, collection-based research
  • Draft precise, well-scoped research questions at each stage of inquiry
  • Identify the type of question being asked and select the source best suited to answer it
  • Distinguish a research map, a research log, and an annotated research outline, and explain where each belongs in the research process
  • Use a research log to document each cycle of research, recording the research question, method, and findings
  • Synthesize research findings into an annotated outline
  • Recognize analytical closure and practical closure, and use the research
  • Explain how the Question Method integrates research and writing

The Role of Questions in Legal Research, Analysis, and Writing

Three terms carry this chapter and everything after it, and if you arrived here without the Introduction, here they are in brief. The legal issue is the problem the law must resolve, the reason the file landed on your desk. The macro question is that issue framed as the single big question your finished document exists to answer: the question presented in a memo, the issue on appeal in a brief. A research question is the micro question you ask and answer along the way, one of the many subquestions whose answers, assembled, resolve the macro question, and it is what this book always means by the term. You never research the macro question directly; held alone, it makes every passage look relevant and buries you. You write it down where you can see it, hold exactly one research question at a time, and let each answer hand you the next. The macro question is the destination; the research questions are the steps. (The Introduction develops this vocabulary in full.)

Research and writing are one iterative process, but early-career researchers tend to split them in two. Research becomes a prewriting phase where you gather everything that might conceivably be useful. Writing becomes a later phase where you sift through the pile. That split reliably produces three problems: you feel overwhelmed about where to begin analyzing the material; you struggle to organize the research and structure the argument; and you discover major gaps too close to the deadline to fix them comfortably.

The Question Method closes that split. Each answer you synthesize becomes a heading in your annotated outline, and findings become the bullets under it. You have started writing and organized your research in the same motion, and you can move to the next question knowing that nothing you found has slipped through the cracks.

Questions are working tools in legal analysis. As you move through a project, asking questions lets you take stock of what you have learned, see where the gaps are, and fold new information into the argument you are building.

The Five Steps and Intermittent Checkpoints

Step 1: Write your research question. State the one thing you need to know right now as a question, in terms that reflect what you currently understand. This is a research question in the Introduction’s vocabulary: a micro question, not the macro question your document will answer. Be specific. A vague question gives you vague results. Your research map, or the answer you just found, will usually supply the question.

Step 2: Research to answer it. Choose the source most likely to answer the type of question you are asking. Search. Read. Track.

Step 3: Write down what you found. In your research log, record your research question, the method you used to attempt to answer it, the source you consulted, what it told you, and your next research question. Add the synthesized answer to your annotated outline.

Step 4: Identify your next question. Based on what you found, what do you still need to know? Write it down.

Step 5: Repeat. Cycle through Steps 1 through 4 until no substantive questions remain, and add any new questions the source raised to your research map.

Checkpoint, Reflect and Evaluate: Before you close out your research, review your work. Are your sources current? Authoritative? From the right jurisdiction? Did your sequence of questions capture the full scope of the issue? Build it as a habit into every cycle, then perform it formally at the end, rather than saving it for a separate phase.

Done Usually Means Done for Now

Step 5 tells you to keep cycling until no substantive question remains, and that is right. It is worth being honest, though, about what “no more questions” usually means in practice. It means no more questions for now. When you take your notes and start turning them into a full draft, you will very often find gaps you could not have seen until you tried to write the argument out. A connection that felt solid turns out to need a case you do not have. An element you were sure you understood raises a sub-question the moment you have to explain it on the page. None of that means your research failed. Research and writing are cyclical, and they feed each other.

So the goal is not to track down every authority you might conceivably need before you write a single word. Chasing that kind of completeness is slower rather than faster, and it runs up a bill your client should not have to pay. The efficient move is to stop once you believe you have most of what you need, start writing, and let the act of drafting show you which gaps are actually worth closing. Stopping for now is a real research decision, not a loss of nerve, even when it leaves you with the nagging sense that there might be a little more out there.

Why This Method Works: Making Your Thinking Visible

The blank page is paralyzing because it asks you to produce something out of nothing. The Question Method replaces it with a prompt. At every stage, you are answering a specific question, and each cycle leaves behind a small, discrete piece of writing. Those pieces pile up in your research log and annotated outline, so by the time you sit down to “write,” your outline is a first draft hiding under bullet points. You will then just be organizing and refining what you have already sketched out.

There is a name for the habit the method trains: metacognition, which means thinking about your own thinking. It is the ability to monitor what you know, recognize what you do not know, evaluate whether your current approach is working, and adjust when it is not. Expert legal researchers are metacognitive all the way down. They keep asking themselves: Do I understand this well enough to move on? Is this source actually answering my question? Have I missed something? Each step of the Question Method cycle corresponds to a metacognitive act.

  • Step 1 (Write your research question) is self-monitoring: What do I currently need to know?
  • Step 2 (Research to answer it) is strategic selection: What is the best way to find this out?
  • Step 3 (Write down what you found) is self-assessment: Did I find what I needed?
  • Step 4 (Identify your next question) is self-regulation: Where should I go next?
  • The Checkpoint (Reflect and Evaluate) is self-evaluation: Is my overall approach working?

Each step asks you to make a judgment, not just perform a task. You are training yourself to think like a researcher.

Why Writing It Down Matters

Writing forces you to be honest about whether you actually understand something. It is easy to read a case and feel like you grasp the rule. It is much harder to state that rule in your own words, in a single sentence, and connect it to the question you asked. If you cannot, you have found a gap, and finding it now beats finding it in the final hours before a major filing deadline. Writing as you go also protects you from collecting a stack of sources and then realizing you no longer remember why each one mattered.

Three Tools, One Process

The method uses three tools that work together. The Research Map is your plan. It lists the questions your writing must answer, each with leads to the sources that might answer it, organized by the logic of your analysis. The research log is your record of the research process. It captures every question you asked, each method you used, all the sources you consulted, and every answer you found, in the order you found them. The Annotated Outline is your record of the research product. It organizes your verified findings by legal issue, so each section lines up with an element, a factor, or a sub-question in your analysis. As you finish research log entries, you move the synthesized findings into the right section of the outline and mark the question answered on your map. One way to keep them straight: the research map tells you where you are going, the research log tells you where you have been, and the annotated outline tells you what you know.

Follow a single question through all three. It is born on the map, often handed to you by a secondary source or by the answer to an earlier question. You work it in the log, sometimes over several entries if the first source only partially answers it. Once you have a verified answer from primary authority, that answer, not the question, goes into the outline, and the question is marked answered on the map. The map holds questions; the outline holds answers.

The Research Map

A research map is a list of the questions you need to answer in your writing to reach a conclusion about how the law applies to your client. You build it over time from the sources you encounter. Questions land on the map from the assignment and the file, from the answers to earlier questions, from sources that answer more than you asked (an encyclopedia entry or a case will often point to several later questions and cite the authority for each), and from your own curiosity about the facts. Next to each question, note any leads: citations you think might answer it, recorded before you have read them.

Add questions as you discover them, but file each one where it belongs in the analysis, so a sub-question sits under the question it narrows. Mark each question open, answered (with a pointer to its place in the outline), or parked, meaning done for now on purpose. Two kinds of questions stay off the map. Questions about your client’s facts go on a separate fact-gathering list, because no database can answer them. And the orientation question you ask just to learn an area’s vocabulary stays in the log, because the map holds only the questions your writing must answer. When you finish one line of reasoning, the map tells you which question to take up next.

The Research Log

A research log captures your process and your results in one place, so you can put the work down and reenter it later without starting over. In law school, research arrives in stable chunks: you get the assignment, you do it, you turn it in. Practice is not like that. You will be pulled off a project midstream for weeks or months, and when you come back, the log is what tells you what you asked, what you found, and what you were about to ask next.  Be kind to your future self, and build the habit of logging your efforts.

One more thing before you build the habit: the entry template below is deliberately overly explicit. It is a scaffold for learning, and eight fields is more than most working researchers keep forever. There is no single right format for a research log. The right format is whatever gets your future self back into the research fastest, and that is personal. Start with the full scaffold, notice which fields are doing real work for you, and adjust until the log fits your process. What cannot be cut is the function: process and results, captured well enough that you can leave the work and return to it cold.

The research log opens with your macro question, written once at the top of your document so you can always check that you have not drifted onto a tangent, and then entries that capture each step in your research with eight fields:

  • Research Question. Write your current question here.
  • Question Type. Definitional, Exploratory, Focusing, or Application (each defined in Types of Questions, below).
  • Method. The research method you used to locate the source you used to answer the question. E.g., secondary source, annotated code, citator, key numbers, search, lead from the map, etc.
  • What I Found. The key information, holding, rule, or definition, in your own words.
  • How This Answers My Question. Does it fully answer, partially answer, or redirect?
  • Source Consulted. Citation.
  • Next Question. What do you still need to know?
  • Notes. Anything else: related sources to check, flags, connections to other questions.

Filling in the Question Type field is not busywork. As you will see, naming the type forces the diagnostic step that tells you which source to consult and roughly where you are in the arc of the research process. The “How This Answers My Question” field does similar work. It makes you say out loud whether you have an actual answer or only a lead, which is the difference between real progress and the comfortable illusion of it.

The Method field matters most when you return to the project after an extended period. Research in practice often has gaps large enough between efforts on a case that memory alone is not enough. The Method field tells you which searches you already ran, including the ones that found nothing, so you do not repeat them, and it lets you answer a supervisor who asks how you found a case. Chapter 9’s closing-out protocol asks you to document what you searched; a log with good Method entries has already done most of that work.

Here is what a completed entry looks like on the page, using a definitional question you will meet again in the Types of Questions section below: what is the federal definition of “organic”?

Field Entry
Research Question What is the federal definition of “organic”?
Question Type Definitional
Method Found in Annotated United States Code (Westlaw) under general
What I Found The statute hands definitional authority for the organic program to the USDA.
How This Answers My Question Partially. The authoritative definition lives in the agency’s regulation, not the statute.
Source 7 U.S.C. § 6502
Next Question What is the USDA’s definition of “organic”?
Notes Definitional question answered at two levels of authority.  7 C.F.R. Part 205 is likely the best next resource

A full example is available in Appendix F.

Reverse Engineering the Question: A Demonstration

To see how legal arguments get built by answering questions, look at this passage from Oakland Police & Fire Retirement System v. Mayer Brown, LLP, 861 F.3d 644, 649-650 (7th Cir. 2017):

In the vast majority of legal malpractice cases, the plaintiff was a client of the defendant-attorney, and the direct attorney-client relationship establishes the attorney’s professional duties to the client. Pelham v. Griesheimer, 92 Ill. 2d 13, 440 N.E.2d 96, 99 (Ill. 1982). There is a small corner of legal malpractice law, however, in which Illinois recognizes that attorneys owe professional duties to persons who are not their clients. In Pelham, the Illinois Supreme Court held that an attorney may owe a duty to a third party when “the primary purpose and intent of the attorney-client relationship itself was to benefit or influence the third party.” Id. at 100. Examples of third parties to whom attorneys owe a duty include third-party beneficiaries of wills, third-party beneficiaries of wrongful death actions, and third-party recipients of formal opinion letters. See In re Estate of Powell, 12 N.E.3d at 21; Geaslen v. Berkson, Gorov & Levin, Ltd. (Geaslen II), 155 Ill. 2d 223, 613 N.E.2d 702, 704, 184 Ill. Dec. 385 (Ill. 1993); McLane v. Russell, 131 Ill. 2d 509, 546 N.E.2d 499, 504, 137 Ill. Dec. 554 (Ill. 1989).

Now replace each sentence with the research question it answers:

  • Under Illinois law, what is the general scope of legal malpractice law? (foundational)
  • Are attorneys ever found to owe a duty to non-clients? (focusing)
  • What are the requirements for that exception to the general rule? (focusing)
  • What are some examples of non-client parties an attorney might owe a duty to? (application)

Notice that for several of these questions the answer comes from a single source, Pelham v. Griesheimer. That is common for settled points of law: elements, familiar principles, conclusions nobody is fighting about. The last sentence is different. It carries multiple citations because the answer requires a survey of different scenarios. When you see one point of law supported by a string of citations, that usually signals a survey question answered by looking across many cases.

Making the questions explicit shows you why some propositions might only need a single citation while others need several.  Here we worked backward from a finished paragraph. When you research, you work forward toward one. Notice the paragraph as a whole serves the macro question of the appeal. Each sentence answers a single research question. Assembled, the small answers resolve the big one, and every piece of legal writing you produce has this structure under its skin.

Finally, those four questions, in order, are a research map. The paragraph is the annotated outline written out in full sentences, each question replaced by its answer and its citation.

Types of Questions

Naming the type of question you are asking is the diagnostic move that makes everything else more efficient. Once you know the type, you know where to look, which source will answer it best, and roughly how far into the cycle you are.

Definitional Questions

Definitional questions ask what a word, phrase, or concept means. For your own orientation, a legal dictionary usually does the job. When the definition is load-bearing for your argument, you need primary authority: the statute’s definitions section, case law construing the term, or agency regulations. The diagnostic question is whether you need a working definition or an authoritative one. “What is the federal definition of organic?” leads you to the statute, which hands definitional authority to the USDA, so your next question becomes “What is the USDA’s definition of organic?” and you turn to 7 C.F.R. Part 205.

Exploratory Questions

Exploratory questions ask what the legal landscape of an area looks like. There are three kinds, and each points to a slightly different source.

  • No-clue questions come up when you have no familiarity with the area at all. You do not know the relevant terms, the key cases, or even whether the issue is mostly statutory or mostly common law.
  • Foundational questions come up when you have a general sense of the area but need to understand the general structure of the law: the elements of a claim, the factors in a test, the major debates, the terms of art
  • Survey questions come up when you need to know how multiple jurisdictions have handled the same issue.

Focusing Questions

Focusing questions ask what the specific legal rule governing your issue is, moving you from the general landscape to the particular standard, test, or sub-rule. This is usually where you cross over from secondary to primary authority. If you have been using secondary sources to get oriented, a focusing question pushes you to go find the actual statute, regulation, or case that states the rule. Focusing questions also breed sub-rules. “What is the standard for reasonable accommodation under the ADA?” leads to “What counts as undue hardship under that standard?”, which is itself a focusing question one level deeper. Every layer of the rule you uncover can expose another layer that needs its own question.

Application Questions

Application questions ask how the rule works once you apply it to specific facts. Like exploratory questions, there are multiple kinds.

  • Fact-finding application questions go looking for cases with facts like your client’s, where the rule was applied in analogous circumstances.
  • Constructive application questions become necessary when no articulated rule yet governs your question, so the applicable rule itself has to be built from several sources, reasoning by analogy from related contexts. When the rule is articulated but no decision applies it to facts like yours, you are still fact-finding: gather the closest decided applications and argue the analogy.

The four types are not a strict sequence. You might circle back to an exploratory question when a focusing question turns up an unfamiliar doctrine. You might answer a definitional question mid-stream when a regulation throws an unexpected technical term at you. Treat the taxonomy as a diagnostic, not a checklist. Its value is helping you recognize, at any moment, what kind of inquiry you are actually in, and therefore where the answer is likely to live. A real case shows how unceremoniously the types trade places.

The Types in Motion: Nix v. Hedden

In Nix v. Hedden, 149 U.S. 304 (1893), a produce importer sued the collector of the Port of New York to recover import duties paid on tomatoes. The Tariff Act of 1883 taxed imported “vegetables” and admitted “fruit” free, and the importer’s argument was that, botanically, a tomato is a fruit. The threshold question is: which tariff category covers a tomato? That turns on a definitional question: how does the statute define “fruit” and “vegetable”? And here the definitional question comes back empty. The Act uses both words and defines neither. The next question is a focusing question, one level up: how have courts defined the terms when applying the Act? The Supreme Court answered that absent some contrary indication, words in the Tariff Act carry their ordinary meaning in common speech, not their botanical or scientific meaning, and dictionaries may aid the court’s memory and understanding but do not control. Now we can ask a closing application question: in ordinary speech, is a tomato a fruit or a vegetable? The Court held, unanimously, that tomatoes are vegetables, served at dinner in, with, or after the soup rather than as dessert, and the duty stood. The Court cited Robertson v. Salomon, 130 U.S. 412 (1889)(which found that beans are vegetables, not seeds, for the purposes of tariffs).

Beginning Your Research: A Practical Start Protocol

Questions are a key part of the research process. Before you get to ask them, it is important that you thoughtfully prepare and plan your efforts. Every research project, simple or complex, begins the same way. Spending a few minutes up front to clarify the assignment and take stock of what you already know pays off enormously later, because it sets the type of your very first question, which sets the source you reach for first.

Clarify the Assignment

Before you open a database, make sure you understand what you are being asked to find. What is the scope? What will count as doing the job well? What are the deadlines, and what resources do you have? If the assignment comes from a supervisor, pin down the jurisdiction, the format they want (a quick email, a formal memorandum, a list of citations), and the depth of research expected. “Do some quick research on this” and “give me a comprehensive memo” are very different requests. This is also where the macro question gets written down. Capture the big question the finished document must answer, in one sentence, at the top of your research log. Also, make sure to note anything you are instructed to avoid. Managing attorneys will often split research tasks among a team of associates, and if you are not tracking the scope of your assignment, you are likely to waste time researching questions assigned to others.

Review the File

Before you search, go through everything you already have. What do the documents tell you about the facts, the timeline, and the parties? It will not always be clear which facts are dispositive for your case. So capture the ones that are helpful to you now. You can revisit the case again as answers make it clear other facts matter. The legal terms you run across (the names of statutes, the titles of the parties, the types of claims) will seed your search terms and will often save you a no-clue exploratory step entirely.

Reflect on Your Current Understanding

Take a moment to be honest about what you already know. How familiar are you with this area? Do you know the relevant terms and claims? Are you an expert who just needs to confirm a couple of points, or someone meeting this area for the first time? That honest self-diagnosis decides what type your first question should be, and getting it right keeps you from wasting time at the wrong level of generality.

Prepare Your Research Strategy

With that assessment in hand, draft your first question. What is the very first thing you need to know? What in the file seems relevant to it? Where might that information live? If you are walking into unfamiliar territory, your first question should be exploratory. If you already have a handle on the landscape and just need a specific rule, it might be focusing. One move can come before even that choice. If you do not yet have the vocabulary the area uses, the terms of art you would need just to search, then your true first step is a plain-language or general search whose only job is to surface those terms. A term of art belongs in a question only after the file supplied it or an earlier step turned it up; it is something your research delivers, never something you assume at the outset. Writing that first question down is itself the first cycle of the method.

When Is Your Research Done? Research Closure

Earlier in this chapter we saw that done usually means done for now. Here is how to make that judgment formally. Research closure is the professional judgment that your research is good enough for its purpose. It is a conclusion you can support with specific, documentable indicators, never just a feeling.

Analytical closure means your research is substantively complete. You have identified the governing rule. You can explain how it applies to your facts. You have found the counterarguments and dealt with them. Your annotated outline covers the full scope of the issue. More research at this point would polish your writing but would not change your analysis.

Practical closure means an external constraint forces you to stop: a budget cap, a client instruction, a supervising attorney’s direction. Practical closure is a fact of professional life. When it arrives before analytical closure, your job is to do three things: document where you stopped and what remains unresearched in your research log; tell your supervisor or client about the limitation; and flag any significant gaps that could affect the analysis.

Practical closures are intentional, not a result of poor planning. Running out of time before a deadline is not a practical closure; it is potential malpractice

One distinction keeps the judgment honest: running out of new sources is not closure. Closure arrives when your outline fully accounts for the legal question and more research would only change your wording rather than your analysis, and it gets finished off with a short ritual, a last sweep of the major sources, a citation update, and a check of the annotated outline for gaps. Chapter 9 builds that finish into a full closing-out protocol.

The method helps with both kinds of closure. Because each cycle is a recorded checkpoint—a question asked, a source consulted, an answer synthesized—you always know where you are. If you reach analytical closure, your research map confirms it by showing no open questions, your annotated outline shows an answer for each, and the judgment is defensible instead of intuitive, because you can point to a research log in which every question has been answered. If practical closure comes first, the questions still open on your map tell you exactly what is left.
