The Question Method in Action: A Full Demonstration

This demonstration runs the Question Method through a problem about student speech and the First Amendment. Read it for the thinking that drives the process, not just the sources consulted but the decisions made at each stage. One decision shapes the whole demonstration: a secondary source appears exactly once, at the start, to orient you and hand you a map. Every answer after that comes from primary authority.

The Assignment

A supervising attorney asks: “Can a public high school principal remove articles he doesn’t like from the school-run student newspaper without violating the First Amendment?” You are a first-year law student. You know the First Amendment protects free speech, but you have never studied how it works inside schools.

Prepare to Research

Before drafting a question, you orient yourself. This is a constitutional issue (First Amendment) in a specific setting (a public school, a student newspaper). You do not know much about this area. You review the file and note that the newspaper is produced as part of a journalism class, that the principal removed several articles before publication, and that the articles do not seem to involve profanity, threats, or illegal activity. There is more detail about the article topics and the timing of the removal, but you are not yet sure which details would bear on the legal question, so you leave them where they are. That self-diagnosis tells you something useful: your first question has to be exploratory. You are not ready to ask what the legal standard is, because you do not yet know which standard governs.

Q1: Explore the Legal Landscape

Question (Exploratory, No-Clue): How has First Amendment free speech protection been applied to students in school environments?

You search the secondary sources in your database for “free speech” and students and schools. In your results, you find a legal encyclopedia entry titled “Students, Constitutional Rights.” The entry states that in Tinker v. Des Moines Independent Community School District, 393 U.S. 503 (1969), the Supreme Court held that students still have free speech rights while at school, and that those rights may be restricted only if the expression materially and substantially disrupts school operations. The entry then surveys the cases marking where that standard does and does not reach. Two matter here. Bethel School District No. 403 v. Fraser, 478 U.S. 675 (1986), permitted a school to discipline lewd and vulgar speech that conflicted with the school’s basic educational mission. And Hazelwood School District v. Kuhlmeier, 484 U.S. 260 (1988), governs school-sponsored publications, with the entry offering the example of a newspaper produced in a journalism class, and permits educators to regulate them when the regulation is tied to “legitimate pedagogical concerns,” a phrase the entry uses but does not define.

Notice how much this single encyclopedia entry hands you beyond the question you are holding. Hazelwood is plainly the branch your facts sit on, and the temptation is to jump straight there. Resist it. The entry told you Tinker is the foundation the later cases build on, and a foundation you skip is a gap you will trip over the moment a later case assumes it. So you do two things at once: you keep one question open, and you map the routes you are not yet taking.

Mapping adding the rest to your research map logging the rest as later questions, with their citations noted as leads, before you can answer any of them. Reading Tinker is clearly next. Hazelwood goes on the map too, but it is not next, because at least one question sits between the first question and the research question Hazelwood will puportedly answer. At least one does: the entry’s description suggests Hazelwood builds on Fraser, so Fraser will need a read after Tinker. You note that in the log.

Example of what our research log entries look like at this stage:

Cycle 1
Research Question: How has First Amendment free speech protection been applied to students in school environments?
Question Type: Exploratory (No-Clue)
Method: Searched the secondary sources in my database for “free speech” and students and schools; opened the encyclopedia entry.
What I Found: Tinker starts the student free speech case law: rights survive the schoolhouse gate, and restriction requires material and substantial disruption. Later cases set context-specific standards. Hazelwood governs school-sponsored publications like a curricular newspaper and ties regulation to “legitimate pedagogical concerns.”
How This Answers My Question: Fully, for orientation. It also identifies which branch of the doctrine governs our facts.
Source Consulted: Legal encyclopedia entry, “Students, Constitutional Rights” [citation] (Note: The entry is a fictitional one for this example)
Next Question: What are the bounds of student free speech rights in school?
Notes: Tinker is the foundational case. Our newspaper is produced in a journalism class, so the school-sponsored branch (Hazelwood) is the thread to pull, but Hazelwood seems to build on Fraser, so Fraser gets a read after Tinker. The entry used “legitimate pedagogical concerns” without defining it; that is a future definitional question.

Those later questions go on your research map, which you keep as a separate document from the log. Here is the map at this stage, with the leads the entry gave you. Your annotated outline is still empty. The exploratory answer does not make it into the outline because the answer came from a secondary source, not a primary one. As you answer each map question from primary authority, its synthesized answer moves into the outline and the question is marked answered.

Note that the future questions are jotted down simply so they can be revisited later. This can be on a separate document, to the side. You can also decide to track them in your annotated outline instead. An example of the annotated outline at this stage, with the other questions, is below. The exploratory answer does not make it into the outline because the answer came from a secondary source, not a primary one. As you progress, you’ll replace the questions with your synthesized answers.

  1. What are the bounds of student free speech rights in school? Leads: Tinker v. Des Moines Independent Community School District, 393 U.S. 503 (1969)
  2. How do courts balance a student’s free speech rights against the school’s basic educational mission? Leads: Bethel School District No. 403 v. Fraser, 478 U.S. 675 (1986)
  3. What free speech protection applies to a school-sponsored student publication? Leads: Hazelwood School District v. Kuhlmeier, 484 U.S. 260 (1988)
  4. What is considered a “legitimate pedagogical concern”? Leads: none yet

One more discipline before you move. One question per question mark. You might be tempted to draft the next question as “What is the Tinker standard, and does it apply to school publications?” That is two research questions sharing a sentence, one about the content of a rule and one about its reach, and each needs its own answer, its own source, and its own log entry. A skilled researcher can hold a bundle like that in her head and unpack it on the fly. You are building the judgment that makes bundling safe later. For now, split them every time.

Q2: Read the Foundation

Question (Focusing): What are the bounds of student free speech rights in school?

You pull up Tinker and find that the Supreme Court held that students do not shed their constitutional rights to freedom of speech at the schoolhouse gate, and that a school may restrict student expression only on a showing that the expression would materially and substantially interfere with the operation of the school or collide with the rights of others. An undifferentiated fear of disturbance is not enough.

Scope the answer to the question and stop. What you also record in your notes is what Tinker was not: the case involved students’ own expression, black armbands worn in protest, not anything the school itself published or sponsored. The rule you just verified is the baseline, and Tinker itself says student rights operate “in light of the special characteristics of the school environment.” That is exactly the balance your mapped Fraser question asks about, so the answer and the map point the same direction. You complete entry 2, , move its answer into your outline, mark the question answered on your map, and Fraser becomes entry 3.

Q3: Clear a Mapped Question

Question (Focusing): How do courts balance a student’s free speech rights against the school’s basic educational mission?

Fraser answers in a paragraph. Reading the opinion, the constitutional rights of students in public school are not automatically coextensive with the rights of adults in other settings, and a school may discipline lewd, indecent, or vulgar speech (there, a sexually suggestive assembly speech) without any showing of Tinker disruption, because a school may insist that speech be consistent with its basic educational mission.

This cycle was short. Write it down anyway. Fast questions are still questions, and the entry takes two minutes. The Hazelwood question becomes entry 4.

Q4: Find the Rule for Your Setting

Question (Focusing): What free speech protection applies to a school-sponsored student publication?

You read Hazelwood. The Court held that a curricular student newspaper is not a public forum unless school officials have opened it, by policy or by practice, for indiscriminate use by the general public or some segment of it. Absent that, educators may exercise editorial control over the style and content of student speech in school-sponsored expressive activities so long as their actions are reasonably related to legitimate pedagogical concerns. The Court distinguished Tinker: whether a school must tolerate particular student speech is a different question from whether it must affirmatively promote it.

That is the governing rule, and it arrives dense with working parts you cannot yet define: “school-sponsored,” a “public forum” opened “by policy or by practice,” “legitimate pedagogical concerns,” “reasonably related.” Each undefined term is a research question. You add them to the research map as sub-questions under the Hazelwood question, ordered to follow the rule’s own structure now that you know it, and then you take them one at a time.

Q5: Define the Threshold Term

Question (Definitional): What makes a student publication “school-sponsored” under Hazelwood?

Stay inside the opinion, because it defines its own term. School-sponsored expressive activities are those that students, parents, and members of the public might reasonably perceive to bear the imprimatur of the school. They may fairly be characterized as part of the curriculum, whether or not they occur in a traditional classroom setting, so long as they are supervised by faculty and designed to impart particular knowledge or skills to student participants and audiences.

A Research Pause: Write the Application into the Outline

Now be careful about a move that feels natural and is easy to get wrong. The next thing your brain wants to ask is: “Is our newspaper school-sponsored?” That is not a research question. No database can answer it, because no database has your file. An Application question in research asks how courts have applied a rule to facts, which sends you looking for decided cases. Measuring the rule against your own facts is a writing step, part of the analysis you will eventually draft.

You do not have to hold that analysis in your head until the memo, though. Take a research pause: stop the research, open the annotated outline, and write the application where it belongs, as a bracketed note under the element it addresses. Here is the outline after this pause. Each question you have answered now appears as your paraphrase of the verified law, with its citation, and the application notes sit in brackets so you can tell your analysis from your authority at a glance. The two questions still open stay on the map, not in the outline.

  1. Students retain free speech rights at school; restriction requires a showing of material and substantial disruption. Tinker (1969). [Personal-expression branch. Not our setting, but the baseline the later cases adjust.]
  2. Student rights are not coextensive with adults’ rights; a school may bar lewd or vulgar speech inconsistent with its educational mission. Fraser (1986).
  3. Educators may regulate the style and content of school-sponsored student expression when the regulation is reasonably related to legitimate pedagogical concerns; a curricular paper is not a public forum unless opened by policy or practice. Hazelwood (1988).
  4. A publication is “school-sponsored” if it is one the public might reasonably perceive to bear the school’s imprimatur: faculty-supervised, curricular, designed to teach. Hazelwood. [Our facts: produced in a journalism class, supervised by a faculty advisor, published under the school’s name. Element almost certainly met; confirm no contrary facts in the file.]

Then resume. The map holds the questions still open. The log holds your questions and answers about the law. The outline is where the law meets your file. Keeping those three documents doing their separate jobs is most of what staying organized means in practice. The legitimate pedagogical concern question on your map is next.

Q6: Define the Operative Standard

Question (Definitional): What is a “legitimate pedagogical concern” under Hazelwood?

The opinion illustrates more than it defines. Educators may act to ensure that participants learn whatever the activity is designed to teach, that readers are not exposed to material inappropriate for their maturity, and that the views of an individual speaker are not erroneously attributed to the school. A school may also decline to sponsor speech that is ungrammatical, poorly written, inadequately researched, biased, vulgar, or unsuitable for immature audiences. In Hazelwood itself, protecting the privacy of pregnant students described in one article, the fairness owed a parent criticized in another, and younger readers’ exposure to frank material all qualified.

A short pause at the outline. Nothing in the file’s account of the removal—articles the principal did not like, contained no profanity, threats, or illegality—maps onto any of those categories, and you note that. The note tells you what to research next. If the school’s stated reasons may fall outside the recognized concerns, you need the outer edge of the standard: the cases schools lost.

Q7: Find the Outer Limits

Question (Application, Fact-Finding): In what circumstances have courts found that a school’s removal of a student newspaper article was not reasonably related to legitimate pedagogical concerns?

Look at the shape of that question. It is an Application question in its research form. It asks how the rule has operated on facts in decided cases, and it sends you to case law, not to your file. You run searches and use the citator to trace how Hazelwood has been treated since 1988, looking specifically for negative or distinguishing treatment, the cases where the school lost. Because your map had no lead for this question, record that route in your log’s Method field. You find that the standard, permissive as it is, does not authorize viewpoint-based censorship or restrictions unconnected to any legitimate educational purpose. Dean v. Utica Community Schools, 345 F. Supp. 2d 799 (E.D. Mich. 2004), is the kind of case this search is built to surface: a federal court held that the student newspaper there had, by policy and practice, become a limited public forum, taking it outside Hazelwood’s deferential standard, and that even measured against Hazelwood, the principal’s removal of a well-reported student article about a lawsuit against the district could not stand.

Checkpoint, the false hit. One search also returns Kennedy v. Bremerton School District, 597 U.S. 507 (2022), a case about a coach’s personal prayer after football games. That case applies a different First Amendment doctrine entirely and does not answer the student-publications question. This is a classic research error, finding a case in the right area that decides the wrong issue. Recording your legal issue and research questions precisely is what allows you to quickly dismiss it without losing time analyzing it

Q8: Take Up the Question Dean Surfaced

Question (Focusing): When is a school-sponsored student publication a public forum rather than a nonpublic forum?

Reading Dean, you learn school facilities are nonpublic forums unless school authorities have, by policy or by practice, opened them for indiscriminate use by the general public or some segment of it. Dean applied that inquiry to a student paper by examining both the written policy and the paper’s actual practice: who exercised control over content, whether students had made the publication decisions in fact, and how the paper had functioned over time.

When the Writing Step Leaves the Database

Now the research requires a pause to uncover how our paper has actually been run. Who has decided what gets published? Has the advisor or the principal exercised control before this incident? Have contributions from outside the journalism class ever been accepted? The file does not say. And no research question will say either, because these are not questions about the law. They are questions about facts you do not have.

Facts you still need go on a task list, and this cycle ends with a call to the client. When the answers come back, they suggest the paper has been run as a nonpublic forum, so you continue researching within Hazelwood’s framework.

Q9: Fill a Gap During Drafting

Question (Application, Fact-Finding, Late Gap Fill): Have courts recognized a legitimate pedagogical concern that justified removing student articles about cultural topics?

This one does not come up during the research phase at all. Later, drafting from the annotated outline, you notice that the removed articles are all about cultural holidays, events, and celebrations. That observation produces a new Application question, and it sends you back for one more targeted case law search, answered, like every question since Q1, from the cases themselves. This is normal and expected. The method treats gaps discovered while drafting as new research cycles rather than as failures of the initial research. Spot the gap, draft the question, add the question to your map, log it, run it.

What the Demonstration Shows

A secondary source appeared exactly once, at Q1, and did the two things secondary sources are for: it oriented you, and it handed you a map. Everything after that was answered from primary authority: Tinker, Fraser, Hazelwood, Dean.  Opinions are the law, and your verified paraphrase of them is what your writing will stand on. At no point did the researcher just type “student newspaper censorship First Amendment” into a search bar. That search would have returned thousands of results and no framework for sorting them.

An Application question in research asks how courts have applied the rule to facts, and it ends in case law. Applying the rule to your own facts is writing, and it lives in bracketed notes on the research outline until it becomes the analysis section of your memo. When that writing step revealed missing facts, the method did not stall. It produced a task list for the client.

The map filled up after one encyclopedia entry but kept expanding when new questions arose out of later stages of research. The log grew by one entry per cycle, in the order you worked. The outline held the answers as found in the relevant authority, most often primary authority, which becomes the Rule and Rule Explanation in most legal writing methods.

Watch also how fast some of the questions resolved. Some questions are so intuitive that you may not even notice you asked and answered one. Write them down anyway. The granularity is scaffolding, and with practice the compression comes on its own. One caution as the compression arrives: a question asked and answered quickly still has to be answered explicitly for the court. An element you satisfied silently is a gap on the page, and working through even the fast questions keeps those skips out of your draft, so you are not left hunting for holes to fill later.

For success in every class. The same discipline scores on a law school exam. Graders award points element by element, and the points most often lost are the ones that felt too obvious to write down. If the hypothetical asks whether the defendant committed first-degree murder and the victim is plainly dead, that element still belongs in your answer: one sentence stating that the victim died and the element is met. You saw it, you counted it as met, but if you did not write it, the point is lost. And every point stacks.

The complete working apparatus of this demonstration, the research map, the full research log, the finished annotated outline, and a case chart of every authority the chain touched, appears in Appendix F, so you can consult the whole record without flipping back through the chapter.

Common Pitfalls

By far the most common mistake is collecting before thinking. It is easy to run search after search and let the sources pile up, and then to look down and realize you are holding a stack of material with no memory of why half of it mattered. Synthesize as you go, writing down what you learn, and you will find yourself better prepared for your full drafts.

A quieter version of the same problem is asking the wrong question. Jumping ahead to an element or factor before you laid the groundwork can leave you with gaps that not only have you backtracking in your research, but also create confusion as you try to grasp the legal argument.

Reflection Point. How might the research have been affected if the researcher had jumped straight to reading Hazelwood before reading Tinker and Fraser?

Another trap is failing to capture the rules and rule explanations you found from your research in your annotated outline, instead capturing only how the rule applies to your facts. In so doing, you are missing key elements needed for your future drafts and making more work for yourself down the road.

Tangents are the last trap. When research turns up sub-issues, it is tempting to chase every tangent the second it appears. The method holds that impulse in check by asking you to record a tangential question on your research map rather than running after it now, keeping your current question in focus until it is answered. Tangents feel urgent because most of them really do bear on the macro question. That is exactly why the map exists. Everything eventually gets its turn; nothing cuts the line.

Chapter Recap

The rest of the book is built on this chapter. The cycle is five steps with intermittent checkpoints: write the question, research it, write down what you found, identify the next question, and repeat, pausing to reflect in every cycle and once more, formally, before you close out. The steps keep you moving, and the checkpoints keep you thinking. Naming each question’s type (Definitional, Exploratory, Focusing, or Application) tells you which source to consult and roughly how far along you are. The research map holds the questions still to answer, the research log records the process, and the annotated outline records the product; together they make your thinking visible, which is the whole point. Closure, analytical or practical, is a documented judgment rather than a feeling. Every chapter that follows simply runs this cycle through a different source.

Key Takeaways

  • The cycle is simple: question, research, synthesize, next question, repeat.
  • The four question types (Definitional; Exploratory, in its No-Clue, Foundational, and Survey forms; Focusing; and Application) each call for different sources and signal where you are in the arc, and the cycle can loop back among them; a definitional question that comes back empty-handed gives you a focusing question about the rule that fills the gap.
  • One question per question mark. A compound question is two cycles. Split it.
  • The research map holds the questions that need to be answered; the research log records the process; the annotated research outline records the product.
  • Application questions in research ask how courts have applied the rule to facts. Applying the rule to your own facts is a writing step, captured in bracketed notes on the outline during a research pause.
  • Analytical closure means the research is substantively complete. Practical closure means an external constraint requires you to stop. Both are legitimate, and both should be documented.
  • Research and writing are iterative. Expect to return to research while drafting, and let the method absorb it.
  • Some questions are so intuitive you may not notice you asked and answered one. Build the detailed habit now, so that when it becomes second nature you can trust you thought critically at every step.

Reviewing Through Practice

The following exercises test the core moves of the Question Method: classifying questions by type, deriving questions from answers, running a complete cycle, and drafting a first question from where your knowledge actually is.

Exercise 3.1 — Name the Type. Classify each of the following as Definitional, Exploratory, Focusing, or Application, with one clause of justification: (a) What does “consideration” mean in contract law? (b) How does the law treat a landlord’s obligation to make repairs? (c) What standard governs a school’s control over a school-sponsored student newspaper? (d) Does any duty to retreat apply inside one’s own home? (e) How do my client’s facts compare to the facts of the leading cases in this jurisdiction? (f) How many states require all parties to consent before a phone call is recorded? (g) How cold did the apartment actually get last January? (h) Why was this statute enacted, and what problem was it built to solve? One of the eight is not a research question at all. Find it and say what kind of question it is instead. Deliverable: the eight labels with justifications, plus the intruder identified. Self-check: the intruder is a factual, investigative question; the law will never answer it, only evidence will. If you labeled all eight with a question type, revisit the distinction between what you research and what you investigate.

Exercise 3.2 — Reverse-Engineer the Paragraph. Take a short paragraph, four to six sentences, from a judicial opinion your instructor assigns, or from any of the school-speech cases named in this chapter’s demonstration. Replace each sentence with the research question it answers, and name each question’s type as you go. You watched this move run in one direction earlier in the chapter; now run it yourself in the other. Deliverable: the sentence-and-question pairs, each question typed. Self-check: read your questions in order, ignoring the original sentences. They should form a coherent chain in which each question could plausibly follow from an answer to the one before. If they read as a disconnected list, some questions are answering the paragraph’s topic rather than its individual sentences.

Exercise 3.3 — One Full Cycle. Choose one question from your Exercise 3.2 chain and run a single complete cycle on it: research it, record the answer, and let that answer hand you the next question. Complete every one of the eight research log fields for the entry, and honor the scoping discipline: the answer you record reports only what your question asked, even if the source offered you the whole landscape. Whatever else the source offered goes in the Notes field as later questions, not in the answer. Deliverable: one complete eight-field research log entry. Self-check: three tests. Could you have written the question with only what you knew at that moment? Does the What I Found entry stay inside the question’s scope? Does the Next Question follow from the What I Found entry rather than from thin air? A no on any test means the cycle needs a rerun, and the rerun is where the learning is.

Exercise 3.4 — Draft the First Question. Three clients, three starting points. Client one hands you a document her former employer wants her to sign that would prevent her from ever suing them, and asks if she should. You do not know what this kind of document is called. Client two asks whether the city can make him tear down the shed he built without a permit; you know words like “permit” and “zoning” but have no grip on how that area of law is put together. Client three asks whether your state requires landlords to provide heat, and you have enough grip to suspect a statute answers it directly. For each client, diagnose your starting knowledge, name the right opening move, and draft only the first question. Nothing else. Deliverable: three diagnoses and three first questions. Self-check: client one’s first move is vocabulary acquisition, and the question you drafted should contain no term of art you do not yet have. Client two’s question should be exploratory. Client three’s can be focusing. And remember the tomato: what you already “know” about a word’s everyday meaning is not knowledge of what the law does with it.

Sample answers for this chapter’s exercises appear in Appendix H.

4

Secondary Sources Research

Secondary sources are materials that explain, interpret, or analyze the law. They help you understand an unfamiliar area and find your way to primary authority. Think of secondary sources as your map of the legal landscape.

Good researchers use secondary sources strategically, both to get oriented early and to deepen their understanding of complex legal frameworks that involve multiple legal authorities. Knowing which type to reach for, and when, is one of the most valuable skills you will build.

Learning Objectives

  • Identify the major types of secondary sources used in legal research
  • Select the most appropriate secondary source for a given type of research question
  • Use secondary sources to locate primary authority efficiently
  • Critically evaluate the authority, reliability, and currency of a secondary source
  • Integrate secondary source research into the Question Method framework

Secondary Sources and the Research Cycle

Secondary sources are the natural home of the early Question Method cycle, and understanding how the method shapes your use of them is what turns a secondary source from a thing you read into a tool you operate.

The type of question you are asking tells you both which secondary source to reach for and how to engage with it once you have it. A no-clue exploratory question sends you to an encyclopedia, where you read for breadth and vocabulary rather than depth. A foundational question sends you to a treatise, where you read for the structure of a doctrine: its elements, its tests, its contested points. A survey question sends you to an ALR annotation or a Restatement, where you read for how authority is distributed across jurisdictions.

The method also disciplines how you engage with a secondary source. So you navigate by table of contents and index, you stop reading the moment your question is answered, and you treat the footnotes as the most valuable part of the page, because the citations they contain are your bridge from the secondary source to the primary authority. Each primary authority a secondary source hands you is a candidate answer to your next question.

Questions This Source Answers

Secondary sources are your primary tool for definitional questions (“What does res judicata mean?”) and no-clue exploratory questions (“I have a contract dispute, where do I even start?”). As your research moves along, they also help with foundational exploratory questions (“What are the elements of promissory estoppel?”) and survey questions (“How have different jurisdictions handled this issue?”). You will rarely use secondary sources for application questions since you will likely ask these long after you have moved beyond a secondary source in your research, but the citations inside secondary sources will flag leads for you to follow later on.

Reflection Point. If secondary sources answer exploratory questions this well, why does Chapter 3’s demonstration stop consulting them after its first question? (A short answer appears in Appendix I.)

How Secondary Sources Are Made and What That Means for Research

Secondary source authority varies enormously. The most well-regarded secondary sources are written by recognized experts: leading professors or practitioners (treatises), Restatement drafters selected by the American Law Institute after extensive vetting, and editorial teams at established legal publishers who keep their work continuously updated.

When you evaluate any secondary source, ask who wrote it, what their expertise is, what the publication venue is, when it was last updated, and who the intended audience is. You might use Wikipedia to get a lay explanation of a concept as background for your search terms, but you would not use Wikipedia, or any unvetted source, to answer your research questions. The reliability of the source determines how much weight you can place on what it tells you and how you might use it in your research.

Types of Secondary Sources in Legal Research

Legal Dictionaries and Words & Phrases

Legal dictionaries, like Black’s Law Dictionary, define legal terms and explain how they are used. Words & Phrases is a specialized tool that collects judicial definitions of specific terms from published opinions, which makes it more powerful than an ordinary dictionary when the precise judicial meaning of a term matters to your argument. Use these at the very beginning, when a term is unfamiliar or its precise judicial meaning is important. If you are reading a case that mentions “mens rea” or “preemption,” check Black’s for a quick definition before diving deeper. At times, they can be used as citable persuasive authority as proof of the common usage of a term with no legal definition

Legal Encyclopedias

American Jurisprudence 2d (Am. Jur. 2d) and Corpus Juris Secundum (C.J.S.) are the two national encyclopedias. Many states have their own, like Texas Jurisprudence, California Jurisprudence, and Florida Jurisprudence. Legal encyclopedias cover nearly every topic with brief summaries of legal principles and citations to leading cases and statutes. Use them at the outset, when you need an overview or do not yet know which specific laws apply.

Encyclopedias give you breadth, not depth. They summarize, but they do not analyze or critique, and they will not tell you which way a contested question is likely to come out. Use one to learn the basic language and framework of your issue, then move on to sources with more depth.

Treatises, Hornbooks, and Nutshells

A treatise is a detailed book written by an expert, often a professor or senior practitioner, that covers one area of law in depth. Some span many volumes; Wright & Miller’s Federal Practice and Procedure fills shelves. Others are a single volume. Hornbooks are single-volume treatise-style texts written mainly for law students. Nutshells are even more condensed and are best for initial orientation rather than deep research.

Reach for a treatise once you have identified your topic and want to understand how the law actually works: the internal logic of a doctrine, how its elements developed, how different courts have approached contested points. If you are studying torts and want to understand how courts analyze “duty of care,” you might turn to Prosser and Keeton on Torts. The author will explain the doctrine, give examples, and cite the most influential cases, and a good treatise will also tell you where the doctrine is unsettled, which is precisely where your hardest research questions will arise.

Restatements of the Law

Restatements are published by the American Law Institute (ALI) to clarify and organize the common law. Each section summarizes a legal rule, explains its reasoning in comments, and cites supporting cases. Examples include the Restatement (Second) of Contracts and the Restatement (Third) of Torts. Restatements are persuasive, not binding, but courts cite them frequently, and some sections carry especially strong persuasive weight, particularly where a state’s highest court has expressly adopted them. Use them when you want the majority rule or a sense of how courts across the country generally handle a specific issue.

American Law Reports (ALR)

American Law Reports sits between encyclopedias and treatises. Each ALR article, called an annotation, focuses on a narrow, specific issue and provides an in-depth discussion supported by extensive case citations from many jurisdictions. If you are researching whether social media posts can support a defamation claim, an ALR annotation titled something like “Defamation Based on Internet Communications” might lay out the general principles, summarize cases from various states, and flag patterns or disagreements among courts.

ALR annotations are best when you have a specific question of law and want to see how it has been treated across jurisdictions, which is exactly what a survey question demands. They summarize cases rather than interpret them, so they do not offer original analysis. Always check when an annotation was last updated, because coverage is selective and an older annotation may miss recent developments.

Congressional Research Service (CRS) Reports

The Congressional Research Service is a nonpartisan office within the Library of Congress that prepares research reports for members of Congress. CRS reports explain complex policy and legal issues in accessible, factual terms, with citations to statutes, regulations, and related legislative materials and nonpartisan context connecting law to real-world policy. They are free at crsreports.congress.gov. Use them when you want the background or policy context behind a federal law. They are not legal authority, but they are an unusually trustworthy, well-cited map of a federal statutory scheme.

Law Review and Journal Articles

Law reviews and journals are scholarly publications produced by law schools, with articles written by professors, practitioners, judges, and sometimes students. They analyze legal issues, propose reforms, and discuss trends and controversies. Use them in the middle or later stages of research, once you understand the basics and want to explore deeper arguments, theories, or policy debates, and use them early when you are doing scholarly writing and need to map the academic landscape. Law review articles can be highly academic, and some lean more toward theory than application, so weigh whether the article actually answers your question or merely contextualizes it.

Practice Guides and Practitioner Materials

Practice guides go beyond the legal doctrine to the practical steps of representing a client in an area of law. They often include forms, checklists, and step-by-step instructions. Examples include the Rutter Group Practice Guides on the West Coast, the Federal Civil Rules Handbook, and state-specific practice guides. Reach for one when you are ready to take your research and put it into practice. If you are helping draft a motion for summary judgment, a practice guide will walk you through the procedural steps, deadlines, and sample language, the “how do I actually do this” answers that doctrinal sources never give.

Other Secondary Sources: Government Websites, Blawgs, and More

Not every useful secondary source comes from a law library or a commercial database. Government websites maintained by courts, legislatures, and agencies are authoritative sources that can offer guidance, explain procedures, and point you to primary authority. The Department of Education, for example, has detailed information on individualized education plans aimed at both families and educators.

Law blogs (“blawgs”) are written by legal professionals, often attorneys or professors, in their areas of expertise. SCOTUSblog posts same-day summaries of major Court opinions; the Volokh Conspiracy covers constitutional and administrative law; Lawfare covers national security. These are valuable for recent developments, but they are not peer-reviewed, so always verify with primary sources.

News, think tank, and nonprofit reports from organizations like the Brennan Center for Justice, the Pew Research Center, or ProPublica provide context, statistics, and public-impact analysis. They are excellent when you are framing a question or trying to understand the social or political backdrop of an issue, but they are not authority.

Searching for Secondary Sources

Once you know which type of secondary source fits your question, you can find it through several paths. LibGuides are research guides created by law librarians nationwide that identify the most authoritative sources in specific areas; to locate one, you can search [topic] research guide.

In commercial databases, Westlaw’s Secondary Sources tab, Lexis+’s Secondary Materials section, and Bloomberg Law’s Secondary Sources collection all let you browse and keyword-search within secondary-source collections. HeinOnline is the most comprehensive database for law review and journal articles and offers historical depth you cannot get elsewhere. For working papers and forthcoming articles, SSRN is invaluable. Google Scholar gives broad coverage but is best used for research outside the legal domain.

In print, use call numbers and subject indexes. Many of the most authoritative treatises are available only in print or through commercial databases, and some are not freely accessible online. When in doubt, ask a law librarian. They are among the most underused research resources available to law students. If you are not sure what asking looks like, copy this: “I’m researching X, and my current question is Y. Which treatise should I start with?”

Answering Questions with Secondary Sources

Matching Source Type to Question Type

The mapping runs from breadth to depth: encyclopedias for no-clue questions, treatises for foundational ones, survey tools for the law across jurisdictions, practice guides when you need to apply the law, and law reviews when primary authority leaves real grey areas. Here it is in one table for reference:

Question type Reach for Example
No-Clue Exploratory Legal encyclopedia Am. Jur. 2d; C.J.S.; a state encyclopedia
Definitional (working definition) Legal dictionary; Words & Phrases Black’s Law Dictionary
Foundational Exploratory Treatise or hornbook Prosser and Keeton on Torts
Survey ALR; Restatement; 50-state survey; CRS report An ALR annotation on your issue
Practical application Practice guide Rutter Group; Federal Civil Rules Handbook
Unsettled or grey areas Law review and journal articles Diane Leenheer Zimmerman, Copyright and Social Media: A Tale of Legislative Abdication, 35 Pace L. Rev. 260 (2014).

Identifying Relevant Materials and Synthesizing Into Your Writing

Navigate a secondary source strategically by starting with the table of contents or index instead of reading cover to cover. Follow citation trails to primary authority, since the footnotes of a treatise section are often as valuable as the text itself. Pull out the key framing, definitions, and synthesized rules, but verify each claim against the primary source it cites, because a secondary source is a starting point, not a substitute for reading the law. Record your findings in your annotated outline as sub-points under the relevant question heading, with citations noted for later drafting.

As you evaluate a secondary source, ask who wrote it, when it was last updated, whether it is the most current edition, and whether it covers your jurisdiction. The most recent edition of a major treatise published five years ago may already be badly out of date in a fast-moving area like data privacy or AI regulation.

Example: Illinois Attorney Malpractice Research

Return to the reverse-engineering demonstration from Chapter 3, built from Oakland Police & Fire Retirement System v. Mayer Brown. Our first question asks, Under Illinois law, what is the general scope of legal malpractice law? (foundational) As a broad background question, we begin with Illinois Jurisprudence. The section on attorney malpractice gives a general overview, noting that an attorney’s duties are generally limited to the client, but there are exceptions , and it cites Pelham. This gives us the foundation to begin mapping out the legal rules in this area of law.

Before we pivot to Pelham to answer the first question for our annotated outline, we mine the secondary source for all the answers it gives, converting them into questions for future research: To whom can an attorney be held liable?  Why might an attorney owe a duty to non-clients? What are some situations where courts have found an attorney to owe a duty to a third party? By logging these future research questions and the sources it cites to answer them, we create a preliminary plan for our research. Once we have logged this information, we are ready to turn to Pelham.

Common Pitfalls

The classic secondary-source error is treating the secondary source as the answer instead of the map.  With rare exceptions, your argument rests on the primary authority the source led you to, not on the secondary source. Follow the footnotes to the primary authority, verifying each claim there.

Be wary of losing yourself in the secondary source for too long. Once you have your foundational understanding and mapped the research questions it shows you, pivot to the primary sources. Otherwise you might find yourself lost on tangents.

Lastly, secondary sources can quietly fall out of date. That currency problem catches people often, so check the edition date and the pocket part before you trust what a source tells you. 

Beyond the Basics

When to Return to Secondary Sources Later in Research

You will sometimes circle back to secondary sources well into a project: when primary authority is vague, and you need persuasive arguments and scholarly context; when you need to find cases in jurisdictions your initial search did not cover; when a tangential issue you did not anticipate turns up; or when a line of cases is hard to follow and you need it broken down more accessibly. If it helps you keep your forward momentum in a research project, it is a valid step.

Citing Secondary Sources in Legal Writing

In most court filings, secondary sources are not cited as authority. In persuasive writing, a Restatement or a leading treatise may occasionally be cited for a proposition that on-point primary authority does not support. In scholarly writing, secondary sources are cited extensively. Know your context. The same source that would be out of place in a brief may be exactly what a law review article needs to establish its scholarly foundation.

Chapter Recap

Secondary sources are the researcher’s roadmap. They orient, explain, contextualize, and point toward primary authority. Each type serves a distinct function keyed to the type of question you are asking. Use them strategically: start broad, go deeper, and always follow the citation trail to primary law. Return to them as needed throughout a project, because the move from secondary to primary authority is not a one-way door.

Key Takeaways

  • Secondary sources explain the law but do not constitute it; they are roadmaps, not destinations.
  • Different types serve different functions at different stages: encyclopedias for no-clue questions, treatises for foundational questions, ALR and Restatements for survey questions, practice guides for practical questions.
  • Always evaluate for author expertise, publication venue, currency, and intended audience.
  • Secondary sources are always persuasive, never binding.
  • Follow the footnotes; secondary sources lead to primary authority.
  • Return to secondary sources as needed throughout the process; the shift to primary authority is not permanent.

Reviewing Through Practice

The hypotheticals that close this chapter and the next three each wall off a single source type so you can drill the moves that source demands before you ever combine them. The first two exercises test your ability to match secondary source types to research situations and to use a secondary source to acquire vocabulary. The third is the drill.

Exercise 4.1 — Match the Source to the Question. Four researchers, four situations. Researcher one has never heard of the area she was just assigned and needs plain-language orientation and vocabulary. Researcher two knows the area exists but needs its full doctrinal structure laid out, with the leading authorities organized. Researcher three needs to know how the fifty states divide on a single narrow rule. Researcher four needs the newest scholarly thinking on an unsettled question no treatise has caught up with. For each, name the secondary source type from this chapter best suited to the job, and one sentence on why.
Deliverable: four pairings with justifications.

Exercise 4.2 — The Vocabulary Harvest. A client’s relationship with her adult son has collapsed, and the son is now refusing to let her see her two young grandchildren. She asks whether a court can order that she get time with them. You do not know what this area of law is called. Spend no more than thirty minutes in a single legal encyclopedia entry, and harvest: every term of art you encounter, a plain-language definition of each in your own words, and where in the entry you found it. Then write the one focusing question the harvest now permits you to ask, a question you could not have written thirty minutes ago.
Deliverable: the term list and the single focusing question.

Exercise 4.3 — Where Do I Even Start? A friend was fired the day after she posted on social media criticizing her manager for refusing to pay overtime. She wants to know whether she has any recourse. A senior associate has asked you to orient the team to the area before anyone touches primary authority: identify the governing framework and the authorities worth chasing. Stay entirely in secondary sources. Do not yet read the statutes or the cases.

Sample answers for the exercises appear in Appendix H.

13 Illinois Jurisprudence § 29:100

5

Case Law Research

Case law is what courts make when judges write and publish opinions explaining how a legal rule applies to a particular dispute. Those opinions interpret constitutions, statutes, and regulations, and where no statute exists, they create common law from the ground up. Engaging with them is not optional; failing to do so can lead to sanctions.

[H]e did not conduct legal research beyond [an older decision on the issue], nor did he reread any of the cases cited to or relied on in . . . the most recent First Circuit decision addressing the contours of [the issue]. Thus, I conclude that [his] erroneous interpretation of the controlling law at the time constituted a breach of the professional duty.

ST Engineering Marine, Ltd. v. Thompson, MacColl & Bass, LLC, 633 F. Supp. 3d 354, 361 (D. Me. 2022), aff’d, [__ F.4th __] (1st Cir. Dec. 6, 2023) (No. 22-1844) [pull the F.4th cite at the check].

Case law only exists for disputes that have actually reached a court, which has a consequence worth sitting with. If you cannot find a case on point, you do not necessarily lack an argument. You may just need to get more creative: reach for persuasive authority from another jurisdiction, reason by analogy from related cases, or lean on the policy reasoning in secondary sources. The absence of a case directly on point is a research finding in its own right. It is not a dead end.

Learning Objectives

  • Explain the role of case law in the U.S. legal system and how judicial opinions are created
  • Identify the components of a judicial opinion and read cases critically
  • Distinguish holding from dictum
  • Use case law research to answer specific research questions within the Question Method framework
  • Use editorial enhancements (headnotes, key numbers, and citators) to guide you to the answers to your next questions
  • Distinguish binding from persuasive case law in a given jurisdiction
  • Update case law using citator tools to confirm the points of law you rely on remain valid

Case Law and the Research Cycle

Case law is where your focusing and application questions get answered. Start with finding. Your question decides your entry point. If you are asking a focusing question (“What is the standard for X?”), the fastest route is usually to let a secondary source or an annotated statute hand you the leading case. The editors have already done the work of identifying the case that states the rule, so there is no reason to run a cold keyword search and sift the results yourself. An application question (“Have courts applied this rule to facts like mine?”) works differently. You take that question to a case that already addresses the issue, then follow only the headnotes, key numbers, and citing references that bear on it, until you reach the factually similar decisions you are after. If there are none, you can try performing narrowing searches covered in Chapter 9.

The method also changes how you read. You read with your current question as a lens instead of giving the whole opinion equal attention. If your question is about the elements of a claim, you read for the court’s statement of those elements and skim the rest. If your question is about how a standard plays out on particular facts, you read for the dispositive facts (the facts the decision actually turned on; Appendix B’s How to Read a Case teaches you to spot them) and the court’s application, and you treat the general statement of law as background. Reading a case to answer a specific question is faster and more accurate than reading a case “to understand it,” because the question tells you what to pull out and what to set aside for later.

And the method shapes what a case gives you. One well-chosen opinion usually answers your current question and raises several more at the same time: a sub-rule you had not seen coming, a cited case that looks even more on point, a distinction that matters to your facts. You synthesize the answer to your current question into your annotated outline and log the rest as next questions, with the citations the case handed you noted right beside them. When the current question is answered and that answer documented, determine what your next question is in the current line of legal reasoning. Refer to your notes to either find sources you have already mapped as addressing this question, or to find your next question if you have reached the end of a line of legal reasoning.

Questions This Source Answers

Case law is your primary tool for Focusing questions about how courts have interpreted a specific rule (“What is the current standard for reasonable accommodation under the ADA in the Ninth Circuit?”) and for Application questions about how a legal standard plays out on particular facts (“Have courts applied this standard to facts like my client’s?”). You will also reach for case law on Definitional questions when a statute’s term has been construed by courts or when the definition is the subject of common law. Case law is usually not your starting point for Exploratory questions. Go to secondary sources first to orient yourself before you dive into opinions, or you will drown.

Reflection Point. Why might a case be a poor source for answering exploratory questions, especially no-clue questions? (A short answer appears in Appendix I.)

How Case Law Is Created and What That Means for Research

Most published opinions come from appellate courts reviewing the decisions of lower courts. Federal district courts publish opinions, but those carry only persuasive weight as they are trial-level courts. Understanding the structure of the court that produced an opinion tells you immediately how much weight to give it, which is why the caption is the first piece of research intelligence in any case.

The Hierarchy of Case Law Authority

Federal courts run in a hierarchy: U.S. Supreme Court, then the Courts of Appeals, then District Courts (the trial level). State systems usually track the same shape: a Supreme Court, one or more intermediate appellate courts, and trial courts.

A case is binding authority only when it comes from a higher court in the same jurisdiction. A case from another jurisdiction is persuasive: it may influence a court, but it need not be followed. An Arizona trial court must follow Arizona Supreme Court decisions. A federal district court in Arizona must follow Ninth Circuit precedent. Both must comply with U.S. Supreme Court constitutional interpretations. An Arizona court may look to California courts for guidance on a question of first impression, but it is not bound by what they decided.

Published vs. Unpublished Opinions

Not every opinion is published in an official reporter. Courts designate some opinions as unpublished (or “non-precedential”), which usually means they are not binding. Rules about citing unpublished opinions vary a lot by jurisdiction. Some forbid it outright, others allow it with specific formatting or caveats. Before you cite any opinion, check three things: whether it is published or unpublished; what your court’s rules say about citing unpublished opinions; and whether the opinion has any subsequent negative treatment. The fact that a database contains an opinion does not make it citable in your jurisdiction.

Searching for Case Law

Secondary sources and annotated statutes are often your best starting points, because they cite leading cases directly and spare you a cold search. Once you have a citation, it becomes the entry point for the question-driven cycle below. Without one, browse by subject using key numbers (Westlaw) or topics (Lexis), or keyword search in Westlaw, Lexis, Bloomberg Law, or Google Scholar; keyword searching earns a larger role for cases than for statutes, because opinions are written in ordinary prose, but a curated finding aid still beats a guessed term. Whichever route you take, the question you are trying to answer dictates the tool, not the other way around. You reach for the retrieval method that best answers the specific question in front of you.

Answering Questions with Case Law

Running the Cycle Through a Case: Question, Read, Synthesize, Repeat

Case law research is the same cycle you run everywhere else in this book, performed one source at a time. Here is the middle of the cycle as a five-move protocol, formatted like Chapter 3’s Five Steps:

Move 1: Bring the question to the case. Locate a case that clearly addresses your question, through a secondary source citation, an annotated code annotation, a colleague’s reference, or a keyword search, and keep the question, not the case, in the foreground.

Move 2: Read for the answer. Read the case critically, with your current question as the lens.

Move 3: Synthesize. Record the answer the case gives to your question in your annotated outline, in your own words.

Move 4: Log the collateral questions. As you read, note the other questions the case answers; you do not have to synthesize them now. Next to each, record the case’s citation and the cases it cites for that point. Those become your research pathways for later questions.

Move 5: Choose the next question. Is it one this case addresses, or one from your secondary source research? The headnotes, key numbers, and citator tools below will connect you to related cases on the same specific point. Research it, and keep the cycle going until every question is answered.

The hardest application questions are the ones no decision squarely answers, and they come in two forms worth telling apart. Sometimes the rule is articulated and what is missing is a decision applying it to a relationship or situation like yours; the work is then analogical, synthesizing the decided applications and arguing which side of the line your facts belong on. And where the rule states its own exception, the argument runs through the rule’s rationale: what is this line for, and does your relationship sit inside it? The condensed worked example later in this chapter, the foster mother as bystander, models that move, and its full chain appears in Appendix G. Other times no articulated rule governs your question at all, and the rule itself has to be built from other sources; that is constructive application.

The foster mother and the “closely related” element. Whether a long-term foster mother can sue as a bystander for negligent infliction of emotional distress comes down to one contested question: is a long-term foster parent “closely related” to her foster child under California law? Thing v. La Chusa (1989) 48 Cal.3d 644 supplies the rule: absent “exceptional circumstances,” the qualifying relationships are those of blood or marriage. No California court has addressed foster parenthood at all, so the argument must be made that the foster relationship warrants the exception. In researching, it is discovered that courts have rejected the in-law relationship, Moon v. Guardian Postacute Services (2002) 95 Cal.App.4th 1005. Is a foster parent more like a parent, or more like a mother-in-law? The answer is genuinely open, and saying so, with the strongest argument for each side, is the research finding. (The full chain, question by question, appears in Appendix G.)

Editorial Enhancements in Case Law Databases

The commercial databases wrap every opinion in an editorial apparatus, and each layer answers a different research question. Learn what each layer is for, and, just as important, what authority it lacks.

Headnotes

Headnotes are brief, editor-written summaries of specific legal points in a case. Each one corresponds to a portion of the opinion. Use them to find the relevant sections quickly and to find other cases on the same point, but never cite a headnote. Only the underlying case text carries precedential authority. A headnote is an editor’s summary; it may not perfectly capture the court’s actual holding, and editors occasionally get it wrong. These can help you navigate to a relevant part of the case and also to navigate to other cases that address the legal point.

Key Number System (Westlaw) and Topic Headings (Lexis)

Westlaw’s Key Number System organizes American law into more than 400 major topics and thousands of subtopics, each with its own key number. Once you find a relevant key number, say “Torts, Negligence, Duty, Foreseeability,” you can pull every case classified under that issue, searching by legal concept rather than by keyword. Lexis offers LexisNexis Topics, which work much the same way. These tools are invaluable for pursuing your next question, because they connect the case you have to the whole universe of cases on the same specific point.

Citators: KeyCite and Shepard’s

Citators track a case’s history and allow you to explore cases connected to the one you are currently reading, both the ones it cites and the ones that cite it. You can use these systems to search through this web for other cases that address the current question in your research cycle by searching within results on the database’s filter sidebar.

These tools show whether the points of law within the case remain valid (colloquially, whether it is “still good law”), how each point has been handled (followed, distinguished, overruled, criticized), and a list of citing references. Learn each system’s color-coded signals: generally red for overruled or serious negative treatment, yellow for caution. A signal is a reminder to read the citing opinions, not a verdict. A case with a yellow signal may have been distinguished on facts nothing like yours, in which case it remains perfectly good authority for your situation. You will only know by reading the citing opinion.

Notice what the citator is actually tracking: points of law, not the case as a whole. Subsequent treatment attaches to specific propositions, which is why both systems tie their analysis to particular headnotes. A case overruled on one point can remain solid authority on another. Plessy v. Ferguson, 163 U.S. 537 (1896), is the canonical example. Brown v. Board of Education, 347 U.S. 483 (1954), repudiated its separate-but-equal doctrine, yet courts went on citing Plessy for unrelated procedural points for decades afterward. So a red flag starts an inquiry rather than ending one: which point of law drew the treatment, and is it the point you rely on? The citing decisions hold the answer.

Identifying Relevant Materials and Synthesizing Into Your Writing

Use headnotes to navigate to the relevant part of a long opinion. Pull the rule out precisely, and quote the court’s articulation of the standard where the exact wording matters. Then synthesize across cases: identify the consistent rule, note the exceptions, and flag any conflicting authority. Record your case law findings in your annotated outline under the relevant question heading, with citation, key holding, dispositive facts, and procedural posture noted. Always note the jurisdiction. A Ninth Circuit case carries different weight in a Ninth Circuit matter than the same case would in a Second Circuit matter.

Common Pitfalls

Case law research has a few traps that catch nearly everyone at some point. The first is treating a headnote as if it were the law. A headnote is an editor’s summary written to help you navigate, and it carries no authority of its own; the opinion text is what you read and what you cite. Just as common is relying on a case without running it through a citator and then learning too late that it was overruled or heavily criticized. A subtler trap is the case that sits squarely in your subject area but actually answers a different question than the one you are holding, which is easy to seize on in relief and hard to dislodge once you have built around it. Quieter still is citing dicta as if it were holding: a court’s aside on a question it was not deciding can read exactly like a rule, but it decided nothing, and opposing counsel will know the difference. Beyond the Basics, below, walks the holding-dictum line. And it is worth remembering that finding an unpublished opinion in your database does not make it citable. Whether you can rely on it depends on your jurisdiction’s rules, so check them before you build on it.

Beyond the Basics

Cross-Jurisdictional Persuasive Authority

When your jurisdiction has little or no case law on an issue, cases from elsewhere can provide persuasive support, especially cases from the same regional circuit or from states with similar statutory frameworks. Look for the jurisdictions whose law most closely resembles yours. A case from a jurisdiction that has adopted the same statutory scheme or the same Restatement section carries more persuasive weight than a case from a jurisdiction built on different foundations.

Borrowed statutes and the neighbor’s answer. A Nevada client wants anti-SLAPP protection for posts made in a small, closed professional listserv, and neither Nevada’s statute (NRS 41.635–41.670) nor any Nevada appellate decision says whether that setting is a protected public forum. The chain that resolves it is constructive application in its clean form. With the home jurisdiction silent, the research surfaces that Nevada modeled its statute on California’s, pegged part of it to California law by amendment, and that the Nevada Supreme Court reads the parallel provisions the way California does, while California has a developed body of forum decisions reaching closed and limited-audience settings. The predicted rule is then built from those sources, the borrowing relationship, the prior-construction canon, and California’s construction, checked against the points where the statutes diverge, and stated as a prediction with its confidence qualified, because a first-impression answer cannot honestly be stated any other way. (The full chain, question by question, appears in Appendix G.)

Opinions, Concurrences, and Dissents, Oh My!

A single decision can contain more than one opinion, and knowing which is which tells you how much weight each one carries. The majority opinion is the one a majority of the participating judges joined, and it is the law of the case. Sometimes no single opinion wins a majority and you get a plurality opinion, the one with the most votes short of a majority. Pluralities bind only as to their narrowest common ground, so read them carefully before you rely on one.

A concurrence is written by a judge who agrees with the result but gets there by different reasoning, or who wants to add something. Concurrences are not binding, but they they can help identify persuasive arguments for future cases.

A dissent is written by a judge who disagrees with the result. Dissents are not binding either, but they can be powerful. When a dissent’s reasoning later becomes the majority view, as happened with comparative negligence (Chapter 1 covers the same shift from the persuasive-authority side) and with the evolution of Fourth Amendment digital privacy doctrine, spotting that trajectory early lets you argue for where the law is heading rather than only where it has been.

A dissent that became the law. In Olmstead v. United States, 277 U.S. 438 (1928), the Supreme Court held that wiretapping a phone line was not a Fourth Amendment search, because federal agents had trespassed on nothing and seized no tangible thing. Justice Brandeis dissented, arguing that the Amendment reaches invasions of privacy, not merely intrusions onto property, and that a rule tied to physical trespass would not survive changes in technology. For nearly forty years that reasoning was persuasive authority and nothing more. Then, in Katz v. United States, 389 U.S. 347 (1967), the Court overruled Olmstead on this point, holding that the Fourth Amendment protects people, not places, and adopting the dissent’s premise. A researcher tracking the trajectory in 1965 would have found the argument that eventually won sitting in a dissent, marked as losing.

Holding vs. Dictum

This distinction does more work in legal argument than almost any other, so it is worth getting right. The holding is the court’s resolution of the specific legal question raised by the specific facts in front of it. Dictum (plural dicta) is everything else the court says along the way: observations, hypotheticals, asides about issues that were not actually presented. Dictum can be persuasive, and dictum from an important court can be very persuasive, but it does not bind anyone.

The test is whether the statement was necessary to resolve the dispute before the court. If the court had to decide it to reach its result, it is holding. If the court could have left it out and reached the same result, it is dictum. Mistaking one for the other is a serious error, because you can end up building an argument on language that no court is actually obligated to follow. When you read a case through the lens of your research question, ask of each useful-sounding sentence: did the court need this to decide the case? Your answer tells you how much you can lean on it.

Chapter Recap

Case law is the backbone of U.S. common law and the place where statutes and regulations get their judicial interpretation. Researching it well means reading cases critically with your research question as the lens, using editorial enhancements to guide you to the answers to your next questions, following citation trails backward and forward, and confirming that the points of law you take from each case remain valid. Dispositive fact identification, knowing which facts actually drove the court’s decision, is the key skill for predicting how a case will apply to new facts. The how-to lives in Appendix B’s How to Read a Case.

Key Takeaways

  • Case law interprets constitutions, statutes, and regulations, and creates common law where no legislation exists.
  • Only cases from higher courts within the same jurisdiction are binding; other cases are persuasive.
  • Run the cycle one source at a time: bring a specific question to a case, read it critically to answer that question, synthesize the answer, log the next questions the case reveals, and use editorial tools to pursue whichever question comes next. The question, not the case, drives the path.
  • Headnotes are research tools, not authority. Only the opinion text carries precedential weight.
  • Dispositive fact identification is the key skill for predicting how a case applies to new facts.
  • Always update cases using KeyCite or Shepard’s before you rely on them, and read far enough to learn which points of law any negative treatment reaches.

Reviewing Through Practice: Did the Store Owe a Duty?

The following exercises test your ability to read a case for a specific answer and to identify its dispositive facts, followed by a longer research problem.

Exercise 5.1 — The Five Moves, Run Once. Bring this question to Hazelwood School District v. Kuhlmeier: what standard governs a school’s control over a school-sponsored student newspaper? Run the chapter’s five-move protocol on it, and keep the question, not the case, in the foreground the whole way. Read for the answer, synthesize it in your own words into an annotated outline entry, and then, this is the move students skip, log the collateral questions: the other questions the opinion answers that are not yours today, each with the case’s citation and the cases it cites for that point. Deliverable: one annotated outline entry answering the question, plus a collateral-questions list with citation pathways. Self-check: your outline entry should be one question’s answer, scoped and in your own words, and your collateral list should hold at least three questions you did not synthesize. If your outline entry tries to summarize the whole opinion, you read the case instead of reading for the answer, and those are different acts.

Exercise 5.2 — Dispositive Facts. Stay with the same opinion. List the facts that, if changed, would change the outcome, and test each candidate with a single sentence: “if instead [the changed fact], then [the different result], because [the court’s reasoning that the change disturbs].” A fact that survives the test unchanged was background, not dispositive. Deliverable: your tested list. Self-check: every entry’s test sentence must point at a specific passage of the court’s reasoning. If a test sentence works without citing the reasoning, you are guessing at what mattered rather than locating it, and guessing is the habit this exercise exists to break.

Exercise 5.3 — Did the Store Owe a Duty? Our client owns a small convenience store. Late one night, a customer was assaulted and robbed by a stranger in the store’s parking lot, and the customer is now suing the store, claiming it should have provided security. The supervising attorney has framed this as a negligence claim and asked you one thing: did the store owe this customer a legal duty to protect her from a stranger’s criminal act? In our state, that duty question is governed by judge-made common law, so your research lives entirely in case law.

Sample answers for the numbered exercises appear in Appendix H.

6

Statutes Research

Case law often explains how judges interpret the law, but statutes are where many legal rules begin. Most areas of modern law, including criminal law, taxation, environmental regulation, employment, and civil rights, are governed by statutes a legislature enacted. Locating, interpreting, and using statutes is its own skill, different from reading cases, because a statute states a rule without explaining itself. One habit will carry you through everything that follows: when you hit an ambiguity, do not groan. Write it down as your next question.

Key Takeaways

  • Describe how statutes are enacted and organized at the federal and state levels
  • Locate statutes using annotated codes in print and electronic databases
  • Read and interpret statutory text, including definitions, cross-references, and exceptions
  • Apply basic canons of statutory construction to resolve ambiguous language
  • Use annotated code features (case notes, cross-references, historical notes) to research a statutory question
  • Update statutes to confirm they remain current and valid law
  • Integrate statutory research into the Question Method framework

Statutes and the Research Cycle

Statutes are where focusing questions about codified law get answered, and the Question Method shapes both how you reach the statute and how you read it once you arrive.

It shapes how you reach the statute because the first fork comes before any searching at all: if you already have a citation, from a secondary source, a case, or the assignment itself, you retrieve rather than search, exactly as Chapter 2’s known-source rule says. When you do not have one, the question type tells you the fastest route to a citation. If your question is “what law governs this regulated activity?”, the quickest path is rarely a keyword search of the code; it is a secondary source or the annotated code’s index naming the controlling section and handing you the citation. Either way, naming the question keeps you out of the frustrating experience of keyword-searching a code and drowning in scattered, decontextualized sections.

It shapes how you read the statute because a statute almost never answers your question in one self-contained sentence. Reading a statute to answer a focusing question means reading the operative provision, the definitions section that controls its terms, the exceptions that might remove your client’s conduct from its reach, and the cross-referenced sections it incorporates. The method’s habit of asking “did this fully answer my question?” is what stops you from relying on a subsection you read in isolation, which is the single most common statutory research error.

The occasions when a statute does answer your application question are those where legislatures draw bright lines, and a bright line performs the application step on its own. Three forms show up constantly. Numeric thresholds: was the complaint filed within the limitations period? Enumerated lists and schedules: is this substance on the controlled-substances schedule? Deeming provisions and safe harbors: a statute voiding any covenant longer than a stated term has already applied itself to every longer covenant. Each of these closes a genuine application question on the statutory text, without a case. Call these self-applying rules, and read the form of a provision early, because its form tells you where the answer lives.

A bright line closes your question only when every operative term is defined, and your facts map onto it without inference, and that condition fails more often than you would expect. A limitations period is a precise number, but whether the clock started is usually a case-law question. A coverage threshold of fifteen employees is precise, but whether a particular worker counts as an “employee” is not. So a self-applying rule does one of two things: it closes the question, or it hands you a candidate answer plus a fresh definitional question, which is the same two-level move that runs from statute to regulation in the organic thread below.
