Chapter 3’s tariff case shows the failure mode. The Act named its categories and defined neither, so the statute could not close anything, and the answer had to come from a court. Had the schedule listed tomatoes by name, the research would have ended at the statute. Same facts, same question type, a different source, because the drafting changed.

Most of all, statutes produce new questions. Every undefined term, every vague standard, every exception, every cross-reference is a potential next cycle. A statute that uses the word “employer” generates the question “how does this statute define employer?” A statute that says “reasonable accommodation” generates “what have courts held constitutes reasonable accommodation?” The method captures these as discrete entries in your Research Log, so the ambiguities you spot become an organized research agenda rather than a vague sense that the statute is complicated.

Questions This Source Answers

Statutes are the right starting point when your question asks what the law requires, prohibits, or permits in a legislatively regulated area. If you need the elements of a federal employment discrimination claim or the procedures for filing a patent application, you begin with the statute. Focusing questions (“What does Title VII require of an employer who receives a complaint of religious discrimination?”) are the most common type you will answer with statutes. Definitional questions come up constantly too (“The statute uses the term employer, so how does it define that term?”). Each annotated-code feature generates potential sub-questions that become entries in your Research Log.

Reflection Point. Why might a statute be a poor source for answering a no-clue exploratory question, even when the area turns out to be statutory? (A short answer appears in Appendix I.)


How Statutes Are Made and What That Means for Research

A statute begins as a bill introduced in a legislative chamber. It is assigned to a committee, marked up, reported for floor consideration, passed by both chambers in identical form (sometimes requiring a conference committee to reconcile differing versions), and signed by the President or Governor. Every stage produces documents that may help you understand what the statute means; these legislative history materials are covered in Chapter 12. The two relevant artifacts that are created in the lawmaking process are session laws and codes.

Session Laws vs. Codes

Session laws are statutes published chronologically as enacted, like the United States Statutes at Large. Codes reorganize session laws by subject into a unified collection, like the United States Code. Annotated codes, such as the United States Code Annotated (U.S.C.A.) published by Westlaw and the United States Code Services (U.S.C.S. ) published by Lexis, are the most useful research tools, because they pair the statute text with editorial enhancements such as case annotations, cross-references, and research aids, turning bare text into a hub that connects to the rest of the legal landscape.

[Figure 6.1] A single statute’s journey from bill introduction through enactment, then how it appears in session laws (chronologically) versus the code (by subject), with the annotated code shown as the research-enriched version.

Searching for Statutes

Start from a citation, drawn from a secondary source or a case, and look it up directly in an annotated code. Without a citation, work through the code’s own finding aids before you reach for a search box. The Popular Name Table locates statutes by their common names (“Clean Air Act,” “Americans with Disabilities Act”) and is often the fastest route when you know what a law is called but not where it is codified. The subject index and table of contents, in print or online codes, sort the code by topic and reward the vocabulary your secondary sources handed you. Keyword searching within statutory databases comes last, not second: statutory drafting is technical and rarely matches the words a searcher would guess, so the search box misses provisions the finding aids surface easily. Save it for when the finding aids fail or when you are hunting a phrase you know the statute uses.

Answering Questions with Statutes: Using Annotated Codes

An annotated code is the statute plus a research apparatus. Beneath the official text, editors stack everything the provision has touched: the cases construing it, the statutes and rules it connects to, its amendment history, the secondary sources discussing it, and citator links. That is why the annotated code, not the bare statutory text, is where statutory research happens; one page holds the law and every pathway out of it. Each annotation type answers a different kind of question, so read what follows as a menu keyed to the question you are holding.

  • Case Notes / Notes of Decisions. Brief summaries of cases interpreting the statute. Read several to spot leading cases or conflicting interpretations. Each is a focusing question a court has already answered about the provision: “how did the court read this element in this context?”
  • Cross-References. Links to related statutes, regulations, or court rules. Each one is a potential research pathway and a potential next question.
  • Historical Notes and Amendments. The legislative evolution of the statute. Critical for figuring out which version was in effect when the relevant events occurred.
  • Research References. Pointers to secondary sources discussing the statute, a shortcut back to treatises and law review articles that may supply the interpretive context you need.
  • Citator Links. Subsequent amendments, cases interpreting the statute, and pending legislative changes. Always run a citator check on any statute you plan to rely on.

A brief worked thread shows the annotations in motion. Suppose your question is the definitional one this book has carried since Chapter 3: What is the federal definition of “organic”? The annotated code’s index points you to the Organic Foods Production Act. The next move is the one experts stop noticing: pull up the code’s table of contents for the Act, chapter 94 of Title 7, and scan its structure. Near the front, where definitions almost always sit, is a section titled “Definitions,” 7 U.S.C. § 6502, and it supplies the program’s defined terms. Reading the operative provisions in context, you find the statute directs the Secretary of Agriculture to establish the certification program, which hands definitional authority to the agency. That delegation is your next question: what does the USDA’s regulation require? The annotations’ implementing-regulations note points you to 7 C.F.R. Part 205, and the cycle crosses into Chapter 7. One statute, read in context, answered the question you brought and told you exactly where the next one lives.

Common Pitfalls

The most damaging statutory error is reading a single subsection in isolation and missing the definitions, exceptions, or cross-referenced provisions that change what it actually requires. Read the section in context every time. The second pitfall is citing the wrong version, since statutes are amended and the language in force when your client’s events occurred may differ from today’s text; the historical notes are there to keep you honest. A third is treating an ambiguity as a dead end rather than as your next question, when in fact an undefined term or vague standard is exactly where the research goes next, into case law, legislative history, or agency interpretation. And do not skip the citator check, because a statute can be amended or held unconstitutional and still sit unchanged in an older database copy.

Beyond the Basics

Relationship Between Statutes and Regulations

Statutes often delegate implementation authority to agencies. The annotated code’s “Implementing Regulations” notes are your pathway to the Code of Federal Regulations, covered in Chapter 7. The full picture of what the law requires usually emerges only when you read the statute alongside its implementing regulations: the statute sets the framework, the regulation supplies the operative detail. Chapter 8 covers  researching across sources in more depth.

Ambiguous Statutes and Legislative History

When statutory text is genuinely ambiguous, courts may look to legislative history (Chapter 12). Identifying and articulating the ambiguity is itself an important skill, because before you can argue that legislative history resolves an ambiguity, you first have to show the text is ambiguous. A court committed to plain meaning will never reach the legislative history at all.

Federal Preemption of State Statutes

When a federal statute preempts state law, the state statute is invalid to that extent. Always consider preemption when you research a state statutory issue in a federally regulated area. The bounds and application of preemption are the subject of a doctrinal course, as it is too complex for the scope of this textbook.

Canons of Statutory Construction

When statutory text is ambiguous, courts do not just guess. They reach for the canons of construction, a toolbox of reading rules courts have relied on for centuries, and knowing the handful that do most of the work helps you predict which reading a court will pick. The plain meaning rule says unambiguous text gets applied as written. Expressio unius says that when a statute lists some things, it probably meant to leave out the rest. Ejusdem generis says a catch-all phrase at the end of a list only catches things like the ones listed. And the rule against surplusage says every word is presumed to be doing a job. (Your legislation or statutory interpretation course will hand you the full toolbox and the fights over when each tool applies. For research purposes, recognizing them is enough.)

Treat the canons the way this chapter has taught you to treat every interpretive tool: as generators of next questions. If two canons point in different directions, which one your jurisdiction’s courts favor is itself a researchable question. Appendix C’s How to Read a Statute carries the full working list with examples; that appendix is where the skill gets its reps. The canons also have a secondary source dedicated to them: Sutherland Statutes and Statutory Construction, the treatise courts themselves cite. Even a browse through its table of contents will give you a sense of the range of canons courts use, and it is the place to turn when a canon starts to matter in your case.

Chapter Recap

Statutes are binding expressions of legislative will. The annotated code is the researcher’s primary statutory tool, connecting legislative text to judicial interpretation, scholarly commentary, and procedural rules. Always read statutes in context, consult the annotations for interpretive case law, and verify currency. And every ambiguity you find is simply your next question.

Key Takeaways

  • Statutes are organized chronologically (session laws) and by subject (codes); annotated codes are the best research tool.
  • Read statutory text in context: neighboring sections, definitions, and exceptions all matter.
  • Annotated code features connect the statutory text to its full legal context.
  • Ambiguous statutory language generates the next Question Method cycle: check case law, legislative history, and agency interpretation.
  • Always update statutes using citator tools.

Reviewing Through Practice

The following exercises test your ability to map a statute’s anatomy and to use annotations as research pathways, followed by a longer research problem.

Exercise 6.1 — Anatomy of a Section. Take a statute your instructor assigns, or choose any substantive section of your state’s code that regulates conduct. Map its anatomy: the operative provision (who must or must not do what), every defined term it uses and where the definition lives, every exception and where it lives, the penalty or consequence provision, and the currency information telling you the text is current. Some of these will live in neighboring sections rather than the one you started in; finding them is the exercise. Deliverable: a labeled anatomy map of the section, with a citation for each component you located outside it. Self-check: if your map shows no defined terms and no exceptions, one of two things is true: you chose an unusually self-contained section, or you did not look in the neighboring sections. It is almost always the second. Statutes are schemes, and reading one section alone is how their traps get sprung.

Exercise 6.2 — Annotations Are Pathways. In an annotated code, take any substantive statute (your instructor may assign one, or reuse a statute you have already read this term) and use its Notes of Decisions or case annotations to identify two cases interpreting the section’s most important term. Do not read the cases yet. Log each in your Research Log as a pathway: the question it appears to answer, the citation, and where the annotation pointed you. This is the discipline of the Chapter 5 protocol’s fourth move, applied from the statute side: annotations hand you leads, and a lead recorded is not an answer synthesized. Deliverable: two Research Log entries, each honestly marked as a lead in the How This Answers My Question field. Self-check: if either entry’s What I Found field states what the case held, you exceeded the exercise. All you know is what the annotation claims, and an annotation is an editor’s summary, not the case.

Exercise 6.3 — Can the Podcaster Record the Call? A client runs a small podcast and wants to record phone interviews with sources. Some sources are in California. The client asks: can I record a phone call with a California source without telling them I am recording? The supervising attorney tells you the governing law is California’s recording-consent statute and asks you to answer from the statute.

Sample answers for the numbered exercises appear in Appendix H.

7

Regulations and Administrative Research

Statutes create broad rules, but much of the law that affects people day to day comes from administrative agencies. Agencies like the EPA, the SEC, and the Department of Labor write the detailed rules, or regulations, that put statutes into practice. Once properly adopted, regulations carry the force of law. You need to be able to locate, read, and update them, because for many regulated activities the regulation, not the statute, is the rule your client actually has to follow.

Learning Objectives

  • Explain the rulemaking process and how regulations acquire the force of law
  • Locate federal regulations using the Code of Federal Regulations and eCFR
  • Read and interpret regulatory text, including authority and source notes and preambles
  • Trace the rulemaking history of a regulation through the Federal Register
  • Update regulations to confirm currency
  • Locate basic state administrative materials
  • Integrate regulatory research into the Question Method framework

Regulations, Administrative Materials, and the Research Cycle

Regulations are where focusing and definitional questions about implemented statutory mandates get answered. A focusing question that begins with a statute (“the statute requires X, so what does compliance actually look like?”) is answered by following the annotated code’s “Implementing Regulations” note straight to the relevant CFR part. A definitional question about a term the statute delegated to the agency (“what does the agency mean by this term?”) is answered by the regulation’s definitions section.

And, like statutes, regulations generate new questions at every turn. An authority note raises the question of whether the regulation stays within its statutory grant. A cross-reference raises the question of what the referenced section requires. An incorporated external standard raises the question of what that standard says. Each becomes a Research Log entry and a next cycle.

Questions This Source Answers

Regulations are your primary tool for Focusing questions about how a broad statutory mandate operates in practice (“What specific requirements must a food manufacturer meet to label a product organic?”) and Definitional questions about terms statutes delegate to agencies (“What is the federal definition of waters of the United States under the Clean Water Act?”). You will also use regulations for Application questions when a client’s conduct is governed by specific regulatory requirements. As you research any regulatory issue, the Federal Register preamble often supplies the interpretive context the regulation’s text alone cannot.

Reflection Point. Why might a regulation be a poor source for answering a no-clue exploratory question, even in a heavily regulated area? (A short answer appears in Appendix I.)

How Regulations Are Made and What That Means for Research

Before a regulation becomes law, it typically goes through a notice-and-comment process governed by the Administrative Procedure Act (APA), 5 U.S.C. § 551 et seq. Understanding the process tells you where to look for interpretive context.

  • Statutory Authorization. Congress passes a statute delegating authority to an agency. The regulation is valid only if it falls within the scope of the authorizing statute, a point that is frequently litigated.
  • Notice of Proposed Rulemaking (NPRM). The agency publishes a proposed rule in the Federal Register, explaining what it intends to do and why. The NPRM includes the proposed text and a preamble laying out the agency’s reasoning.
  • Public Comment Period. The agency takes written comments from the public, including individuals, companies, and advocacy groups. Comments come in through regulations.gov and become part of the rulemaking record.
  • Final Rule. After considering comments, the agency publishes the final rule in the Federal Register with binding text, a detailed preamble responding to significant comments, and an effective date.
  • Codification. The final rule is codified in the Code of Federal Regulations (CFR), organized by subject.

Every stage produces documents you can use. The NPRM shows original intent. The comments show what stakeholders objected to. The final rule’s preamble explains why the agency made specific choices. That preamble is often your single best interpretive resource, the regulatory equivalent of legislative history, and overlooking it is the most common way researchers misread what a regulation requires. A shorthand worth keeping: NPRM = intent; comments = objections; final-rule preamble = the agency’s answers.

Organization of Federal Regulatory Publications

Federal regulations move through a fixed set of official publications, and each answers a different question about the same rule: what has been proposed, what is codified, and what is current today. Knowing which publication holds which answer is most of regulatory search strategy.

  • Federal Register (FR). The daily publication of proposed rules, final rules, notices, and executive orders. Best for tracing recent or proposed regulatory action.
  • Code of Federal Regulations (CFR). The annual codification of all effective regulations, organized into 50 titles by subject. The official source for existing federal regulations. Structure: Title, then Chapter (agency), then Part, then Section. For example, 40 C.F.R. § 50.8 is Title 40 (Protection of Environment / EPA), Part 50 (National Ambient Air Quality Standards), Section 8 (carbon monoxide standards).
  • List of CFR Sections Affected (LSA). A monthly record of which CFR sections have changed since the last annual update. Use it to verify currency when you are working from a print or annual edition.

Two small features of every CFR part deserve attention now, because later chapters lean on them. The authority note cites the statute that empowers the agency to issue the part. The source note cites the Federal Register document that created or last amended it, which is the thread you pull to reach the preamble. Appendix D’s How to Read a Regulation shows both in context.

Searching for Regulations

If you already hold a CFR citation, retrieve it directly in the eCFR and skip the search. Otherwise, start from the statute or topic: use the annotated code’s “Implementing Regulations” notes to locate the relevant CFR part. If you know which agency regulates your issue, browse that agency’s chapters in the CFR or check the agency’s website, which often links directly to its regulations. Keyword searching, in the eCFR (free, updated daily), Westlaw, Lexis, or GovInfo.gov, comes last here too, and only with the precise terms the regulation uses, identified through secondary sources or the statute itself; regulatory drafting is at least as technical as statutory drafting, and the CFR’s structure is the better guide.

Answering Questions with Regulations and Updating for Currency

Regulations can be amended, suspended, or invalidated at any time. Currency checking matters even more for regulatory research than for statutory research, because agencies revise regulations far more often than legislatures amend statutes.

  • The eCFR (ecfr.gov). Updated daily; the most current freely available source. Each title carries a note indicating the date through which it has been updated.
  • List of CFR Sections Affected (LSA). Use it when you are working from the annual CFR edition to catch any intervening amendments.
  • Federal Register. Check for recent proposed rules, interim final rules, and final rules that may affect your regulation. The Unified Regulatory Agenda, published twice a year, lists the rules agencies plan to propose or finalize.
  • Citators. Run KeyCite (Westlaw) or Shepard’s (Lexis) on your CFR section. Regulatory citators identify decisions that have interpreted, applied, challenged, or invalidated the regulation. A regulation struck down by a court is no longer enforceable, even if it still appears in the CFR text.
  • Agency websites. Some agencies post compliance bulletins, guidance updates, or enforcement-policy changes that effectively change how a regulation operates in practice before the CFR text is formally amended.

[Figure 7.1] Regulation Currency Checklist: (1) check eCFR; (2) check the LSA; (3) check the Federal Register; (4) run KeyCite or Shepard’s; (5) check the agency website.

State Administrative Materials

Each state has its own administrative code and rulemaking process. State registers publish proposed and final rules; state codes compile them by subject. The research process mirrors the federal model: locate the authorizing statute, find the implementing regulation, and check for updates. Westlaw and Lexis often provide better search functionality than state agency websites do.

Common Pitfalls

Stopping at the CFR text without checking the Federal Register for the preamble and context is the most common regulatory research error, because the agency’s explanation of what its rule means lives in the preamble, not the codified text. The next is ignoring amendments, since agencies revise regulations frequently and an older copy can be silently out of date. Watch too for overlooking related parts that hold the definitions and exceptions controlling the section you are reading. And keep guidance documents in their place; treating a non-binding guidance document as if it were a binding regulation can lead you to tell a client something is legally required when it is only the agency’s preference.

Beyond the Basics

Guidance Documents: Influential but Not Binding

Policy statements and guidance documents do not go through notice-and-comment and lack the force of law, but they are highly influential as statements of how agencies interpret and enforce their regulations. Chapter 13 covers these materials, the weight courts give them, and the case law that governs that weight, in depth.

One more development belongs on your radar now: in 2024 the Supreme Court ended the longstanding practice of courts deferring to an agency’s own reading of an ambiguous statute. The doctrinal ins and outs belong to your admin law course. What the shift means for how you weigh administrative authority is addressed in Chapter 13.

Chapter Recap

Regulations implement statutory mandates through the APA notice-and-comment process and carry the force of law once properly promulgated. The Federal Register records rulemaking; the CFR organizes final rules. The preamble is often as important as the regulation text itself. Always trace a regulation’s statutory authority, rulemaking history, and currency, and let your unanswered questions, not the bare CFR text, tell you when you are done.

Key Takeaways

  • Regulations implement statutory mandates through the APA notice-and-comment process.
  • The CFR codifies current federal regulations; the eCFR provides the most current free version.
  • The Federal Register preamble is the most important interpretive resource for understanding regulatory intent.
  • Authority notes link regulations to enabling statutes; source notes link them to Federal Register history.
  • Always verify currency using the eCFR, LSA, Federal Register, and citators.
  • Post-Loper Bright, courts independently interpret ambiguous statutes rather than deferring to agency interpretations.

Reviewing Through Practice

The following exercises test your ability to locate a regulation, verify its currency, and trace its statutory authority, followed by a longer research problem.

Exercise 7.1 — Locate and Verify. Chapter 3’s Research Log example asked what the federal definition of “organic” is. Finish the job properly. Locate the current federal regulation governing organic labeling in the eCFR, and then run the full five-step currency checklist from Figure 7.1 on the section you found: the eCFR check with its updated-through date, the LSA if you touched an annual edition, the Federal Register sweep for pending or recent rules, a citator run, and a pass through the agency’s website for guidance that changes how the rule operates in practice. Deliverable: the regulation’s citation, and a currency note recording what each of the five steps found, including the exact date the eCFR title had been updated through on the day you checked. Self-check: a currency note with five entries reading “nothing found” is possible but should make you suspicious. At minimum, the eCFR step always yields a date, and the Federal Register step nearly always yields activity somewhere in the part. If your note is empty, your searches were too narrow.

Exercise 7.2 — Trace the Delegation. Every regulation exists because a statute told an agency to write it. Find the authority note for the federal organic-labeling regulation (Appendix D shows you where the authority citation lives in a regulatory section), name the delegating statute, and write two sentences: one on what the statute itself decided, and one on what it left for the agency to fill in. This is the statute-to-regulation joint that Chapter 8 will build on, met here for the first time in the wild. Deliverable: the authority citation and your two sentences. Self-check: the two sentences should describe genuinely different things. If they blur together, you have not yet located the line between the legislature’s judgment and the agency’s detail, and that line is the whole reason this source type exists.

Exercise 7.3 — Does the Roofer Need Fall Protection? Our client is a small residential roofing contractor. A new hire will be working on a two-story house, roughly twenty feet up. The client asks: am I legally required to provide fall protection, and if so, at what height does the requirement kick in and what counts as acceptable protection? The supervising attorney tells you this is governed by the federal OSHA construction regulations and asks you to answer from the regulations.

Sample answers for the numbered exercises appear in Appendix H.

8

Researching Across Sources

Research is rarely a tidy matter of finding one statute and a few cases. Often the law governing a client’s issue is spread across many sources: a federal statute, a state common law rule, agency regulations, local ordinances, decades of judicial interpretation. To research efficiently, you have to understand how those sources intersect and when to move from one to another. To research completely, you have to do something more: identify every authority type that governs the issue, reach each one, and read it directly rather than through another source’s description of it. A researcher who treats each source type as a separate, sealed exercise produces a fragmented analysis. A researcher who lets one source type stand in for the others produces something worse, a confident analysis with a hole in it. This chapter teaches both skills: moving fluidly among sources while keeping a single legal question in view, and knowing, at the end of a complex project, that your coverage of the governing authority is complete.

Chapters 4 through 7 treated each source type on its own. This chapter shows how they work together in practice, with the Question Method as the organizing framework.

Learning Objectives

  • Recognize when a legal issue is governed by multiple overlapping sources of law
  • Build a research map that identifies every authority type in play, and maintain it as a coverage checklist
  • Apply the Question Method to move across source types as each answer hands off to the next question
  • Read statutes and regulations directly, including their definitions and related sections, rather than only as other sources characterize them
  • Recognize and document research gaps
  • Use a return pass through secondary sources to confirm coverage on complex, wide-ranging projects

Researching Across Sources and the Research Cycle

In single-source research, the Question Method tells you which source to consult and how to read it. In multi-source research, it does something more: it tells you when to switch sources and why. The mechanism is the “Next Question” field of your Research Log. Each source you consult answers your current question and, in doing so, generates the next one, and the next question may be answered best by a different source type.

A secondary source orients you and points you to the governing statute. The statute states the framework and, through an undefined term or a delegation of authority, points you to the regulation. The regulation supplies the operative detail and, through its ambiguities, points you to the case law interpreting it. The case law applies the rule to facts and may send you back to a secondary source to make sense of a confusing line of decisions. Each handoff is a question type changing: exploratory to focusing, focusing to definitional, definitional to application. Being explicit in your method lets you flag when you have gotten all you can out of a source and need to move forward.

The method also decides which source comes next. The question in front of you picks the source, and the discipline of asking “did this fully answer my question, or only partially?” after every cycle keeps you moving wherever the answer actually lives. What the full landscape of authority contains, and how to be sure you covered all of it, is the work of the rest of this chapter.

The Statute-Regulation-Case Law Triangle

In many areas of law, statutes, regulations, and case law all together set the law. The statute sets the framework and delegates authority to an agency. Regulations implement the statute’s mandate with technical and procedural detail. Case law interprets both the statute and the regulations, resolving ambiguities and setting their scope. Your research has to engage all three to give a complete answer. Missing any one of them leaves a gap, and often an error, because the missing source is frequently the very one that would have changed the conclusion.

[Figure 8.1] Triangle diagram with Statute, Regulation, and Case Law at the three points and double-headed arrows between each pair labeled “authorizes/implements,” “interprets/applies,” and “interprets/applies.”

Reflection Point. Chapters 4 through 7 walled you into one source type at a time. Why does that discipline make multi-source research easier rather than harder? (A short answer appears in Appendix I.)

Go to the Source: Reading Primary Authority Directly

Multi-source research creates a temptation that single-source research never did. When the cases you are reading quote the statute, it begins to feel as though you have read the statute. When a guidance document walks through the regulation, it feels as though you have read the regulation. You have not. You have read someone’s excerpt, selected for someone else’s purposes, and building your analysis on an excerpt is one of the quietest ways a multi-source project goes wrong.

Consider what an opinion’s quotation of a statute actually gives you: the language that mattered to that dispute, and nothing more. The definitions section that controls the operative terms almost never makes it into the quote. Neither do the exceptions, one of which may remove your client’s conduct from the statute’s reach entirely, nor the cross-referenced sections the provision incorporates. The quoted text may not even be current. Statutes get amended, and an opinion faithfully quoting the pre-amendment version will mislead you without containing a single inaccurate word. And a court’s paraphrase is not text at all; it is interpretation, valuable for what it is and dangerous the moment you mistake it for the thing interpreted.

Regulations invite the same shortcut and punish it the same way. Guidance summarizes. Preambles explain. Opinions excerpt. The operative CFR text is what binds your client, and the sections surrounding it in the same part, the definitions, the exemptions, the compliance detail a neighboring section supplies, are part of the rule whether or not any other source thought to mention them.

So adopt a bright-line habit: the moment a statute or regulation becomes load-bearing in your analysis, open it in the code and read it the way Chapters 6 and 7 taught. For a statute, that means the operative provision, the definitions section, the exceptions, and the cross-references. For a regulation, it means the section itself, the part’s definitions, the authority note, and the adjacent sections of the part. (Appendices C and D walk both reading protocols step by step.) Then make your Research Log honest about what happened. An entry that records the statute as a case quoted it is a case-law entry. The statute gets its own entry when you have read the statute.

The Single-Source Trap

Open your Research Log and suppose you find five consecutive case-law entries. Is something wrong? Quite possibly not. If your question is a common-law question, the elements of negligence, the formation of a contract, the cases are where the rule lives, and a long run of case entries is the research working exactly as designed. The same is true when you are deep in application questions, comparing your client’s facts against a line of decisions, or following a citation trail through the cases that built a doctrine. A streak in the log measures the rhythm of your research. By itself, it tells you nothing about whether the research is complete.

The failure that deserves a name is a coverage failure: an authority type that governs your issue and that you never looked for, never found, or never read directly. Call it the single-source trap, and notice that it comes in two forms. In the first, a governing authority type is missing from the research altogether. The agency’s regulations sit unread while you work through a sixth case, or a local ordinance controls the client’s conduct and never appears in your log.

In the second, subtler form, the authority type appears in your log but only secondhand: every statutory point you hold arrived through a case’s characterization, every regulatory point through a guidance document, and the primary text itself was never opened. That is the failure the last section taught you to correct. You will see the trap again in Chapter 9’s diagnostic toolkit and in Chapter 14’s practice contexts.

Answering Questions Across Multiple Sources

Each source type answers a different kind of question about the same issue. The statute tells you the framework. The regulation tells you the technical detail. The case law tells you how both operate in practice. Your annotated outline should reflect all three, and your Research Log should track when you shifted from one source type to another and why.

Worked example: ADA website accessibility. Must a business make its website accessible under the ADA? One question, three source types, each answering what only it can. A secondary source orients and points to 42 U.S.C. § 12182, Title III’s public-accommodations provision; the statute, read directly, supplies the framework but never mentions websites, because it was enacted in 1990; the DOJ’s Title III regulations, 28 C.F.R. Part 36, turn out to contain no web-accessibility rule, only guidance and proposals, which makes the guidance’s weight its own question; and the case law reveals a circuit split on whether websites are “public accommodations” at all. The synthesis is an annotated outline holding all four findings, framework, regulatory gap, guidance and its weight, split, which is what an accurate answer to a genuinely contested question looks like.
The full walkthrough appears in Appendix G.

Managing the Research Log Across Multiple Sources

When you research across sources, the Research Log becomes especially important. Each source type may generate a new sub-question that leads down a different path, so record each question, source, and finding to keep track of how the sources connect. Two small habits make the log do coverage work for you. First, note the authority type of each entry (statute, regulation, case, secondary source, guidance), so that you can quickly see if you have yet to directly engage with a source. Second, when you switch source types, record why in the Next Question field. That “why” is the record of your research judgment, and it is what lets you, or the colleague who inherits the project, reconstruct the movement across sources later.

When Sources Conflict

When a federal statute and a state common law rule appear to cover the same conduct, ask first whether the federal statute preempts state law expressly or impliedly. If it does not, both may apply, and your analysis has to address each. The priority rules in practice: the U.S. Constitution overrides all other authority; federal statutes and treaties override conflicting state law; regulations are valid only if within statutory authority; and case law interprets all of the above and cannot contradict clear statutory, regulatory, or constitutional text.

Confidence on a Complex Project: The Return Pass

There is a question every new researcher asks on a big project, usually late at night: how can I know I have found everything? The issue sprawls across a statute, two sets of regulations, a stack of guidance, and case law from three jurisdictions. The log runs to dozens of entries. No search can prove a negative. The anxiety is reasonable. Confidence on a complex project is something you build, with specific moves.

Audit the research log, authority type by authority type, direct engagement or explicit ruling-out, as the trap section described. Consider going back to the secondary source that opened the project and reread the relevant sections now, at the end, with everything you have learned. Call it the return pass. Now you know the legal points, so additional sections might prove relevant where they were not before. Looking at these sections will hopefully confirm what you have found in your research. If nothing new surfaces, that silence is affirmative evidence that your coverage is complete, and it belongs in your Research Log as exactly that: “return pass through [the treatise], §§ [sections]; no new threads.” Checked and found nothing missing is a finding. It is what earned confidence looks like on paper. In the worst case, if you find another authority you did not realize was implicated or new research questions you had not asked, you have highlighted a gap in your research and you address it the same way you would any gap you surface, regardless of when you find it.  

Whatever the result, you will be more confident when you reach the close out phase of your research process.

Common Pitfalls

Complex research projects are subject to more error not because they are necessarily novel problems, but rather because the size of the project leads to more moments for small failures to compound. You did not validate a point of law and traced backward to other cases that cited that faulty point of law. Or you took a case’s overview of a statute as good enough, and in failing to look at the statute directly, did not find critical language in the section that changes the application of the law to your case, leading you to continue down a line of cases that are not on point for your purposes. The solution is to research thoughtfully and not to skip your research pauses. By engaging in this way, you are more likely to catch a misstep, misinterpretation, or gap that would cause you trouble later unchecked.

Another trap of complex projects is losing yourself in the research. There are so many authorities across various source types that you may continue searching for more and more to feel as though you have truly ‘completed’ the research. To avoid this, stick to the method and synthesize what you find into your outline. In the same way you would not look for 5 cases to cite to establish the elements of negligence in a common law research problem, more is not always better in a complex research project. By synthesizing as you go, you can best assess if you need more sources to either securely support a legal claim in your writing or address a gap in your argument.

Chapter Recap

Multi-source research is the same cycle with two added judgments: when to switch sources, and how to know your coverage is complete. The research map supplies the checklist, and the statute-regulation-case law triangle is its regulatory core. Read every load-bearing statute and regulation directly, related sections included, because an excerpt is not the law and a paraphrase is someone’s interpretation of it. The single-source trap, properly scoped, is a coverage failure, a governing authority type unreached or reached only secondhand, and its diagnostic is the map, not the streak. On sprawling projects, build confidence structurally: audit the log against the map, run the return pass through your secondary sources, and triangulate your finding tools. The line between “the law is unsettled” and “I have not finished looking” remains the difference between a finding and a confession.

Key Takeaways

  • Most real legal issues are governed by an overlapping combination of statutes, regulations, case law, and constitutional or local provisions.
  • The research map identifies every authority type in play and, kept current, becomes the coverage checklist you audit against.
  • Read statutes and regulations directly, with their definitions, exceptions, and related sections; never rely on another source’s excerpt or paraphrase for a load-bearing rule.
  • The single-source trap is a coverage failure, an authority type unchecked or checked only secondhand; a streak of same-type log entries is not by itself a problem.
  • The return pass, rereading your orienting secondary source at the end with expert eyes, catches unpulled threads cheaply and turns “nothing new” into documented evidence of coverage.
  • Research gap identification, distinguishing “the law is unsettled” from “I haven’t found the answer yet,” is a critical professional skill.

Reviewing Through Practice: The Drone Capstone

The following exercises test your ability to map the relationships among source types, to read primary authority directly, to distinguish unsettled law from incomplete research, and to run the return pass, followed by the capstone of the drill set.

Exercise 8.1 — Build the Triangle. Return to the organic-labeling question that has run through this book since Chapter 3, and build its research map as a statute-regulation-case-law triangle. At the statute corner, record what the delegating statute decided, located through the regulation’s authority note. At the regulation corner, record what the regulation itself supplies, located through the eCFR. At the case-law corner, identify at least one decision interpreting either, located through an annotated code or a citator. Then annotate each edge of the triangle: what question sends a researcher traveling from this corner to that one? Deliverable: a one-page triangle map, corners filled, edges annotated with the traveling questions. Self-check: the edges are the exercise. If your map has three full corners and empty edges, you have catalogued sources without mapping the movement between them, and movement between sources is what this chapter teaches.

Exercise 8.2 — Go to the Source. Pick any case from your Part I drills that quotes or paraphrases a statute (the Chapter 5 or Chapter 6 drill lines work well). First, write down the rule as the opinion presents it, using only the opinion. Then pull the statute itself in an annotated code and read it the Chapter 6 way: operative provision, definitions section, exceptions, cross-references, and a currency check on the text. Deliverable: a list, three items minimum, of what the code delivered that the opinion’s excerpt did not, a controlling definition, an exception, a cross-referenced requirement, an amendment postdating the opinion, plus one sentence on whether any item changes how you would state the rule. Self-check: if your list is empty, reread the definitions section; it is almost never empty. And if nothing on the list changes your statement of the rule, say why. That conclusion is a finding too, and you could only reach it honestly by doing the reading this exercise drills.

Exercise 8.3 — Unsettled or Unfound? From any research question you have worked in this course (any of the Part I drills works, or your instructor may assign one), write two findings: one that honestly reads “the law is genuinely unsettled on this point,” and one that honestly reads “I have not yet found the answer.” Defend each in two sentences, naming what you searched and why that search entitles you, or does not, to the stronger claim. “The law is unsettled” is a professional conclusion; “I could not find any law” is a confession. Knowing which one you are holding is the skill. Deliverable: the two findings with defenses. Self-check: the unsettled finding must rest on a search thorough enough that a supervisor would accept it, and its defense should say so specifically. If both of your findings could trade labels without the defenses changing, neither defense is doing its job yet.

Exercise 8.4 — The Return Pass. Return to the secondary source that opened any completed drill (the Chapter 4 drill is a natural choice) and reread the sections relevant to that drill’s question, now, with the whole chain behind you. Deliverable: either one unpulled thread, written as a properly drafted next research question with a note on which source type would answer it, or a logged finding that the return pass surfaced nothing new, naming the specific sections reread and the legal points you checked them against. Self-check: “nothing new” is a legitimate answer, and a vague one is not. If you cannot name the sections and the points you tested them against, you have not run the pass; you have just reread the introduction.

Exercise 8.5 — The Drone Question. Our client runs a small real-estate marketing business and wants to start flying a camera drone to shoot aerial photos and video of listed properties, for paying clients. The client asks: what do I have to do to operate the drone legally for this commercial work, and what legal exposure should I worry about? You do not yet know the area, and no one has narrowed it for you. Map and answer the question across whatever sources govern, using the Question Method to move among them.

Sample answers for the numbered exercises appear in Appendix H.

9

Narrowing and Closing Out Your Research

Chapter 2 introduced the opening phase of search: natural language searching, synonym mapping, and the core Boolean connectors. This chapter picks up where that process leads, at the moment your questions become specific and your goal shifts from discovery to precision.

At this stage you know the legal terminology and you know which sources govern. Your remaining questions are targeted: Does the rule apply in this specific factual context? Is there a circuit split on this point? Has this particular statutory phrase been interpreted by courts in my jurisdiction? Those questions call for search tools built for precision, not exploration. This chapter also takes on the question every new researcher struggles with: when am I done? The narrowing tools and the closing-out protocol are two halves of the same final phase. One finds the right authority, the other confirms you have all of it.

Learning Objectives

  • Construct precise Boolean searches using proximity, exclusion, and frequency connectors
  • Transition from broad orientation searching to precise, targeted retrieval as your questions narrow
  • Apply a systematic closing-out protocol to confirm research is complete, current, and gap-free
  • Diagnose and respond to common research problems: no results, too many results, conflicting authority, circular trails
  • Document the research closure decision in a defensible, professional manner

The Full Connector Set: Proximity, Frequency, and Exclusion

Building on the core connectors from Chapter 2, this chapter adds the precision tools that make Boolean searching a real narrowing instrument.

Proximity connectors specify how close together terms must appear, which dramatically cuts false positives. /n requires terms within n words of each other in either order (landlord /5 heat, within five words). /s requires terms within the same sentence. /p requires terms within the same paragraph. Be aware that /s and /p do not look for terms within sentences or paragraphs as we think of them structurally. They are database shorthand for a default number. For example, /s might be shorthand for /14. What the actual number is a database-specific artifact they don’t share.

Databases also allow you to determine distance between terms as well as the order. pre/n requires the first term to appear before the second within n words (reasonable pre/3 accommodation). Platforms also allow you to use connectors to exclude results where terms are within a distance from each other (e.g., cases where medical is within three words of liability), but the syntax is not uniform. At the time of publication, Lexis uses term NOT /n term while Westlaw uses BUT NOT (or %) term /n term. Check the system’s documentation before attempting to use proximity exclusion.

Frequency connectors. ATLEAST n(term) requires the term to appear at least n times in the document. ATLEAST3(preemption) retrieves only documents where preemption is discussed substantively rather than mentioned in passing. This helps you find documents where a concept is a major focus rather than a stray reference.

[Figure 9.1] Side-by-side comparison of the same research question searched with AND alone versus with proximity connectors, showing how proximity sharply reduces results while improving relevance.

Translating Your Research Question into a Precision Search

The discipline of the Question Method pays off here, because a precisely stated question already contains the terms and relationships you need for a Boolean search. This is the most direct link between the method and the mechanics: a well-drafted question is, in effect, a Boolean search waiting to be formatted.

Example. Your question is “Have Ninth Circuit courts held that remote work is a required reasonable accommodation under the ADA for employees with anxiety disorders?” Extract the components: core concepts (ADA, reasonable accommodation, remote work / telecommute / work from home, anxiety); the relationship (accommodation and remote work should be close together); the jurisdiction (Ninth Circuit filter). The resulting search: “reasonable accommodation” /10 (remote! OR telecommut! OR “work from home”) AND (anxiety OR “mental health”), with the Ninth Circuit filter applied.

Now look at the same question translated badly: ADA AND accommodation AND remote AND anxiety. Every concept is welded to a single word, so the telecommuting cases vanish, and nothing requires the concepts to appear anywhere near one another, so a passing mention of anxiety in an unrelated ADA opinion still qualifies. Set the two side by side and you can see what the good version is actually doing: synonyms gathered with OR, concepts bound with proximity, jurisdiction filtered. A bad example next to a good one is the fastest way to learn the difference.

The general principle: find all synonyms for a concept using OR and the expansion operators; require concepts to co-occur using AND; specify how close together they must appear using proximity connectors; find specific legal tests using quotation marks; exclude irrelevant context using NOT; require substantive treatment using ATLEAST; and require term order using pre/n.

Database-Specific Connector Syntax

Westlaw, Lexis+, and Bloomberg Law use similar but not identical syntax. Westlaw uses /n for proximity; Bloomberg uses W/n; Lexis uses /n but with slightly different behavior for /s and /p. Always check the syntax documentation for the specific database before you run precision searches. A misread connector can produce a radically different result set, and worse, it can silently exclude the very authority you are looking for. You can find each database’s terms and connectors in their help file.

Building a Search Log

For any substantial project, record each search string, the database used, the number of results, and whether the search was productive. A search log keeps you from repeating yourself, provides evidence of thoroughness, and supports your research closure documentation. It works hand in hand with the Research Log: the Research Log tracks the questions and answers, the search log tracks the exact queries that produced them. As your research log evolves with your the growth of your research skills, so too will your search log.

The Closing-Out Protocol

Precision searching narrows your results; closing out confirms that what you found is complete and current. Notice that this is the Question Method’s Checkpoint again, run at full scale: where the checkpoint asks whether one answer was sufficient, the protocol asks whether the whole project is.

  • Run a final sweep. Before you declare research complete, run at least one more search using different terms than the ones that produced your best results, and at least one search aimed deliberately at authority that cuts against your position, the adverse-authority question Rule 3.3 will hold you to (Chapter 10). These additional searches, even when they turn up nothing new, create a documented record that you searched comprehensively.
  • Update every case and statute. Run KeyCite or Shepard’s on every primary authority you plan to cite, and read each one for the specific proposition you cite it for, because you never cite what you have not read. For cases, check for negative treatment in your jurisdiction, and note which points of law it reaches. For statutes, check for pending amendments and recent session laws. For regulations, check the eCFR effective date and the LSA.
  • Evaluate the outline for gaps. Every legal proposition should be supported by primary authority, and every ambiguous term should have a source addressing it. A gap is either a remaining research question or a genuine area of legal uncertainty your analysis must acknowledge.
  • Document the closure decision. Record that you completed the protocol. Note the databases searched, the final search strings, the dates you verified authorities, and any open questions. This protects you professionally; if your research is ever questioned, you can show exactly what you did and why you concluded it was enough.

Reflection Point. Your annotated outline already answers the question. Why does the closing-out protocol still make you run a search aimed at authority that cuts against you? (A short answer appears in Appendix I.)

The Difference Between No Results and No Law

A search that returns nothing means one of two things: there is no authority on the point, or your search was too narrow. Before you conclude the law is silent, try alternative terms, remove proximity constraints, expand to persuasive authority from other jurisdictions, and check secondary sources for discussion of the issue. Only after you have exhausted the alternatives is a “no results” conclusion defensible, and the difference matters enormously, because “there is no law” is a powerful argument while “I could not find any law” is a confession. (This is the same line Chapter 8 drew for research gaps; here it becomes a closing-out discipline.)

When Research Goes Wrong: Diagnosing Common Problems

Every researcher hits moments when the research is not working. What sets competent researchers apart is that they diagnose the problem accurately and apply a specific correction instead of just searching harder.

  • No results. Check whether your terms are too specific, and broaden with OR and synonyms, remove proximity constraints, or reduce the number of terms. Check whether the issue is genuinely novel and may call for analogous authority. Check whether you are in the wrong source type, the single-source trap from Chapter 8, and should return to a secondary source.
  • Too many results. The search is probably too broad. Add a proximity connector, add a third term, apply jurisdiction and date filters, or use ATLEAST for substantive treatment. Remember that you are looking for the answer to a specific question, not every possibly relevant document.
  • Conflicting authority. Diagnose whether the conflict is jurisdictional, factual, temporal, or doctrinal. Apply the hierarchy of authority. Address the conflict honestly in your analysis; a genuine split is itself a research finding, not something to hide.
  • The law is genuinely uncertain. Before you conclude the law is unsettled, verify with secondary sources, search for analogous authority, and check persuasive authority from other jurisdictions. Law review articles, Restatement provisions, and legislative history give you the analytical framework for genuinely open questions.

[Figure 9.2] Diagnostic flowchart: “What symptom are you experiencing?” branching to No Results, Too Many Results, Conflicting Cases, No Authority in Jurisdiction, and Can’t Understand the Source, each leading to a first diagnostic step and a recommended response.

Common Pitfalls

The temptation at the finish line is to declare victory the moment you find your best authority, but skipping the final sweep with alternative terms is how a controlling case slips past you. A quieter danger lives in the syntax itself: a mistyped connector does not error out, it simply returns the wrong set, sometimes excluding the very authority you needed, so verify the database’s connector rules before you trust a precision search. Be careful, too, not to read an empty result list as proof the law is silent; “I could not find it” and “it does not exist” are very different claims, and only the second is an argument. Finally, do not leave closure undocumented. The note explaining what you searched and why you stopped is what protects you if the work is ever questioned.

Chapter Recap

Narrowing and closing out are the final phases of every research cycle. Precision Boolean searching converts broad orientation results into a targeted, comprehensive authority set. The closing-out protocol confirms that every authority is current, every gap is identified, and the research decision is documented. Together they convert “I think I’m done” into “I can demonstrate that I’m done.”

Key Takeaways

  • Proximity connectors (/n, /s, /p), platform-specific exclusion operators, ATLEAST, and the exact-phrase quotation are the primary precision instruments.
  • Translate your research questions directly into Boolean searches: key terms become search terms, and the relationship between them determines the connectors.
  • Closing out requires a final sweep with alternative terms, citator updates on all primary authority, gap analysis against the annotated outline, and documentation of the closure decision.
  • Document the closure decision in your Research Log.
  • No results is not the same as no law; exhaust alternative search strategies before concluding the law is silent.

Reviewing Through Practice

The following exercises test precision search construction and the discipline of documented closure. Each one writes to the Search Log.

Exercise 9.1 — Precision Translation. Take three research questions from a current project, or from your instructor’s set, or from your own completed drills, and translate each into a precision search: synonyms gathered with OR, concepts bound with proximity connectors rather than bare ANDs, jurisdiction and source type filtered before the search runs. Record every search in your Search Log as you go: the query as entered, the filters, the result count, and one line on result quality. Deliverable: three Search Log sequences, one per question. Self-check: every search string should use at least one proximity connector doing real work. If AND is the only connective tissue in all three strings, you translated the Chapter 2 way, and this chapter existed to upgrade you.

Exercise 9.2 — The Bad Translation. Take this question: is a landlord liable when a tenant’s guest is injured on a broken staircase? Translate it twice. First, deliberately badly: all ANDs, single words, no synonyms, no proximity, no filters. Then properly, with the full Chapter 9 toolkit. Run both versions back to back and write two sentences on what the two result sets tell you. Contrast is the fastest way to sharpen the discrimination this chapter teaches, and building the bad version yourself teaches more than being shown one. Deliverable: both strings, both result counts, and your two sentences. Self-check: your sentences should name a specific difference, in volume, in relevance of the first page, in what floated to the top, not a generic “the good one was better.” If you cannot name the specific difference, look at result ten in each list and start there.

Exercise 9.3 — The Closing-Out Run. Run this chapter’s closing-out protocol, every step, on any research chain you have completed in this course (a Part I drill works well), and document the closure decision in your Research Log: the final sweep with alternative terms, the citator updates, the check against the original question, and your explicit finding of analytical closure or a named remaining gap. Deliverable: the documented closure decision. Self-check: one test, and it is the chapter’s test. If you cannot document why you stopped, you have not closed. You have just stopped.

Sample answers for this chapter’s exercises appear in Appendix H.

II

Part II: Advanced Legal Research Topics

Part I introduced the process. Part II provides the knowledge to apply everything you have learned to more complex and varied legal research processes. The sources get more specialized, the contexts more demanding, and the stakes more explicitly professional.

Chapter 10 comes first for a reason. Research quality is not a private matter between you and your grade. It is a professional obligation with rules attached, and lawyers are sanctioned for failing it. Reading the ethics chapter before the specialized materials sets the frame for everything that follows: the verification habits, the citator checks, the Research Log as a reviewable record. Chapter 10 will show you that these are the method’s own closing steps, performed with the rules of professional conduct in view.

Chapters 11 through 13 add sources most first-year courses never reach: dockets and court documents, legislative history, and the deeper administrative materials, including where agency interpretations stand after Loper Bright. Each follows the familiar template, so you will know exactly what to ask of it. Chapters 14 through 17 then shift from sources to contexts. Litigation, transactional, and compliance practice each bend the method to their own rhythms. Generative AI tools demand the cycle’s verification discipline more than any source before them. The NextGen bar exam tests the skills this book has been naming all along. And scholarship hands you a new kind of macro question, one where the thesis is the answer your paper exists to defend.

Unlike Part I, this part does not need to be read straight through. After Chapter 10, take the chapters as your coursework or your practice demands them.

10

Ethics and Professional Responsibility in Legal Research

Legal research is a professional obligation, not just a technical skill. The quality of your research bears directly on your clients’ outcomes, the integrity of legal proceedings, and your standing as a licensed professional. A missed case is not just a lower grade. In practice it can be a forfeited argument, a sanctioned attorney, and a harmed client.

When an appellant provides no citation to legal authority supporting his contentions, those contentions are waived.

Shields v. Town of Perrysville, 136 N.E.3d 309, 312 n.2 (Ind. Ct. App. 2019).

Learning Objectives

  • Identify the professional responsibility rules that govern legal research obligations
  • Recognize the consequences of inadequate legal research for clients, courts, and counsel
  • Apply the duty of competence and the duty of candor to common research scenarios
  • Evaluate the ethical dimensions of using AI and emerging technologies in legal research
  • Describe the standards courts use when sanctioning attorneys for research failures

The Duty of Competence: Model Rule 1.1

ABA Model Rule of Professional Conduct 1.1 requires lawyers to provide competent representation, which includes “the legal knowledge, skill, thoroughness, and preparation reasonably necessary for the representation.” Comment 8 speaks directly to technology: a competent attorney must keep abreast of “changes in the law and its practice, including the benefits and risks of relevant technology.”

Competent legal research means knowing which sources govern, locating them efficiently, reading and synthesizing them accurately, and updating them to confirm they are still valid. Failing to find controlling authority is a breach of the duty of competence, not merely an oversight. Relying on outdated or overruled authority can amount to incompetent representation. The duty demands the current, controlling answer. Recall the following excerpt from Chapter 5.

[H]e did not conduct legal research beyond [an older decision on the issue], nor did he reread any of the cases cited to or relied on in . . . the most recent First Circuit decision addressing the contours of [the issue]. Thus, I conclude that [his] erroneous interpretation of the controlling law at the time constituted a breach of the professional duty.

ST Engineering Marine, Ltd. v. Thompson, MacColl & Bass, LLC, 633 F. Supp. 3d 354, 361 (D. Me. 2022), aff’d, No. 22-1844 (1st Cir. Dec. 6, 2023) [pull the bound F.4th cite at the check].

This case shows the core competence failure: the attorney relied on outdated authority without checking whether the law had moved. The closing-out protocol’s second step—updating every case and statute through a citator (Chapter 9)—is designed to prevent exactly this. And the finding held up: the First Circuit affirmed, so the competence holding is not one trial court’s view of how much research is enough. The habit the method builds is the same habit the rule requires.

The Duty of Candor Toward the Tribunal: Model Rule 3.3

Rule 3.3 prohibits false statements of law to a tribunal and requires disclosure of directly adverse controlling authority the opposing party has not cited. That creates an affirmative research obligation: you must search for controlling adverse authority, not only authority that helps you. Rule 3.3 also prohibits citing false or fabricated authority, a concern of heightened urgency in the era of AI-generated research.

The rule reaches authority that is in the controlling jurisdiction (not just persuasive authority from elsewhere), directly adverse (not merely unfavorable or tangential), and known to the lawyer, which includes authority you should have found through competent research. Ignorance born of inadequate research is no defense. The duty of candor therefore reaches back into the quality of your research. You cannot disclose adverse authority you never found, and “I didn’t find it” is no answer when competent research would have.

The Duty to Supervise: Model Rules 5.1 and 5.3

Supervising attorneys are responsible for making sure subordinate lawyers and non-lawyer staff comply with the conduct rules. In research, that means senior attorneys must review delegated research for accuracy, completeness, and proper citation. The Research Log serves a professional function here. It documents what was searched, what was found, and why the researcher concluded the research was complete. If a supervisor questions your work, the log gives a complete, defensible record. If you are the supervisor, the log is what lets you actually evaluate a subordinate’s research rather than simply trusting it.

Consequences of Inadequate Legal Research

Sanctions by courts. Courts have broad sanctioning authority when counsel fails to research adequately. Sanctions include monetary penalties, attorney fee awards, adverse rulings, and public reprimands in published opinions.

[B]ecause I find that Defendants incurred these unnecessary fees in major part due to inadequate legal research on the part of Plaintiffs’ counsel, I find that both Plaintiffs and their counsel are jointly and severally liable to Defendants.

Royal Oak Ent., L.L.C. v. City of Royal Oak, 486 F. Supp. 2d 675, [pincite] (E.D. Mich. 2007).

Waiver of arguments. Courts routinely hold that arguments unsupported by citation to legal authority are waived. Inadequate research does not merely weaken an argument. It can forfeit it entirely, as the Shields court’s note at the opening of this chapter makes plain.

Malpractice exposure. An attorney who fails to find controlling authority and thereby harms a client’s case may face a legal malpractice claim. The competence failure that begins as a research shortcut can end as a civil judgment against the lawyer.

AI and Emerging Technology Considerations

[A]t this point, no lawyer should be using ChatGPT or any other generative AI product to perform research without verifying the results. Period.

In re Martin, 670 B.R. 636, 649 (Bankr. N.D. Ill. 2025).

Generative AI tools produce output that can look authoritative but may contain fabricated case citations, incorrect legal propositions, and misattributed quotations. Courts have sanctioned attorneys for submitting briefs citing AI-generated, nonexistent cases. The professional responsibility implications are clear: the duty of competence requires verification of all research, whatever its source. AI tools do not relieve attorneys of the obligation to confirm that cited authorities exist, say what they are claimed to say, and remain valid law.

Emerging court rules and bar guidance increasingly require disclosure when AI tools are used to generate any part of a brief or memorandum. Watch your jurisdiction’s developing rules. Chapter 15 addresses AI tools and legal research in depth; this section supplies the ethical framework that governs their use.

Build Verification Into Your Research Workflow

As you research, keep a running citation log: every source you intend to cite, with its full citation and the database where you verified it. Before you finalize any document, run every case citation through KeyCite or Shepard’s to confirm that the points of law you cite it for remain valid. Before you finalize any statute citation, verify the current text against the eCFR or the official code and check for pending amendments. If you used any AI tool at any stage, independently verify each source it identified before it goes into your work product. These are not separate ethics chores bolted onto research. They are the closing steps of the Question Method, performed with the awareness that the rules of professional conduct require them.

The chapter in one glance:

Rule The research failure it targets The method step that prevents it
Rule 1.1 (competence) Stale or overruled authority; citations that do not support the claim they are offered for; inadequate or no research at all The citator update and the read-before-you-cite check in the closing-out protocol
Rule 3.3 (candor) A one-sided search that misses adverse authority The adverse-authority search in the closing-out protocol’s final sweep (Chapter 9)
Rules 5.1 and 5.3 (supervision) Delegated research no one can review The Research Log as the reviewable record

Common Pitfalls

The competence failures that get lawyers sanctioned tend to share a shape: relying on the first answer rather than the current, controlling one. Leaning on an old case without running it through a citator is the classic version, and ST Eng’g Marine is what it looks like in a published opinion. A second pitfall is researching only your own side; Rule 3.3 obligates you to find and disclose directly adverse controlling authority, so a one-sided search is not just weak advocacy, it is an ethics problem. A third is trusting AI output without verification, since a fabricated citation is a Rule 3.3 violation whether it came from a chatbot or anywhere else. And do not treat the Research Log as busywork; it is the record that protects you and your supervisor if the quality of the research is ever questioned.

Chapter Recap

Research quality is an ethical obligation with names attached. Rule 1.1 demands competent, current research; Rule 3.3 demands candor about adverse authority; Rules 5.1 and 5.3 make supervisors answerable for research they delegate. Courts enforce all of it through sanctions, waived arguments, and malpractice exposure, and AI has raised the stakes on verification rather than lowering them. And here is the good news: the citator step, the adverse-authority question, the Research Log—the habits this book has been building in you all along—are exactly the habits the rules require.

Key Takeaways

  • Legal research is a professional obligation, not merely a technical skill.
  • Model Rule 1.1 (competence) and Model Rule 3.3 (candor) are the primary rules governing legal research.
  • Courts sanction attorneys for inadequate research; arguments unsupported by authority may be waived.
  • AI tools create heightened verification obligations; they do not substitute for professional research judgment.
  • Supervising attorneys are responsible for the research quality of their subordinates.
  • The Research Log documents your process; it is your professional record of diligence.

Reviewing Through Practice

The following exercises test your ability to connect the professional conduct rules to specific research practices: verification, adverse-authority searching, and documentation.

Exercise 10.1 — The Sanctions Autopsy. Pull ST Engineering Marine; the competence excerpt in this chapter gives you the citation. Read the court’s account of what the attorney failed to do, and then rebuild this chapter’s closing table from the opinion itself rather than from memory: for each failure the court names, write the rule it violates and the method step that would have prevented it. Deliverable: your rebuilt three-column table, failure, rule, preventing step, with a pincite to the opinion for each failure. Self-check: compare your table against the chapter’s. Where they differ, the difference is the finding: either you caught a failure the chapter’s table generalized over, which is good reading, or you attributed to the opinion something it does not say, which is the exact error the autopsy exists to catch.

Exercise 10.2 — The Adverse-Authority Question. Return to any completed drill from Part I and run the one question your first pass almost certainly skipped: what controlling authority cuts against my conclusion? Draft the question, run the search, and log the result in your Research Log even if the search comes back empty, because Rule 3.3 does not accept “I didn’t look” as an answer. Deliverable: the Research Log entry, including the searches run. Self-check: an empty result logged with its searches is a passing answer. An empty log is not. The difference between those two is the whole of the candor duty’s research obligation.

Exercise 10.3 — The Verification Pass. Run a full verification pass on any completed drill from Part I: build the citation log, one line per source you would cite, run every case through a citator, and confirm every statute and regulation’s currency against the official or current source. Deliverable: the citation log with a verification status for every line. Self-check: every line has a status and a date. The log you produce is not homework. It is the record that answers for you when someone asks whether the research was done, and Rules 5.1 and 5.3 mean someone is allowed to ask.

Sample answers for this chapter’s exercises appear in Appendix H.

11

Dockets and Court Document Research

Court documents give you a window into how the law operates in practice. Judicial opinions tell you what courts decided; the pleadings, briefs, and motions show you how lawyers argued, what facts they marshaled, and how courts reasoned through contested issues before issuing a published opinion. Court document research is especially valuable for finding arguments in cases with similar facts, understanding the procedural history of a precedent-setting case, locating unreported orders, and mining briefs for citation lists in novel areas. Court documents are a research tool, not a source of authority, and keeping that distinction clear is the first discipline of this chapter.

Learning Objectives

  • Explain what a court docket is and what types of information it contains
  • Locate federal court dockets using PACER and free alternatives
  • Identify and retrieve key court documents: complaints, briefs, motions, and orders
  • Evaluate the research value of court documents and their limitations
  • Locate state court documents using state-specific resources
  • Integrate court document research into the Question Method framework

Court Documents and the Research Cycle

Court documents occupy an unusual place in the Question Method. They are almost never the answer to a research question, but they are one of the most efficient accelerators of the research cycle. Understanding how the method treats them prevents the beginner’s error of citing a brief as if it were authority.

The method shapes how you find court documents, because they are most useful once a specific question has already narrowed your inquiry. Early exploratory questions are answered by secondary sources, not dockets; reaching for PACER before you know the governing standard wastes money and time.
